The starmap stage additionally offers Grid depth, whose skirt the system scene does not implement. The GM's 2D orrery has no falloff concept at all, so nothing is missing there either. Not an [[A32]] or [[A40]] repeat. **WHAT HAS NOT BEEN SEEN: the crossfade in motion.** The Browser pane composites only intermittently here, so the two levels, their opacities, the decade handover and the exact AU positions were all read off the live scene numerically rather than watched. Someone should zoom the 3D system view in and out once and say whether the subdivision reads as one continuous grid or as a shimmer. Related: [[G4]], [[G5]], [[A32]], [[A37]]. | built v2.1.386-beta |
| G9 | **Show the campaign's OWN other stars in the 3D system view, at their true direction, brightness and colour — so a GM can build constellations.** Proposed 2026-08-03. The existing generic starfield STAYS and becomes the backdrop behind them: these are the real charted systems drawn in front of it. **HIGHLY FEASIBLE — every input already exists and none of it is new physics.** (1) DIRECTION: the starmap holds true 3D positions for every system since the rebuild, so the direction from the viewing system to any other is a vector difference. (2) BRIGHTNESS: `luminositySolar` is already derived (`physics/stellar-evolution.ts`), and apparent magnitude is `M + 5 log10(d/10pc)` with distance already computed depth-aware. (3) COLOUR: a star-colour derivation already exists. **AND IT SIDESTEPS THE HARD PROBLEM ENTIRELY:** these are FAR-FIELD objects drawn on the celestial sphere at effective infinity, so they need a DIRECTION and nothing else — no huge coordinates, no interaction with the floating origin, no precision question of the kind [[A19]] spent a session on. **TWO ANCHORS THAT MAKE IT SELF-CHECKING, and both are famous:** Sol seen from Alpha Centauri (4.37 ly) should come out around magnitude **0.5** — a first-magnitude star, which is the well-known fact that our Sun appears as a bright star in Cassiopeia from there; and Sol from Tau Ceti (11.9 ly) around magnitude **2.6**, a solid naked-eye star. If the model reproduces those two it is behaving. **REUSE, and this is where it will go wrong:** take the colour from the EXISTING star-colour derivation and the luminosity from `luminositySolar` — do NOT add a class-to-colour table or a second luminosity calculation. That is the recurring fault in this codebase and a hardcoded palette for "what colour is a K dwarf" has been reverted once already. **Questions to answer rather than assume.** How many are worth drawing — the bundled map has 42 systems, which is sparse, so most of the sky remains the generic backdrop and that is fine; should very faint ones be culled by magnitude? Are LABELS wanted, since a constellation without names is a field of dots, and the starmap already has a label pipeline that could be borrowed? And is this player-view only as proposed, or does the GM view get it too — one implementation either way. **THREE DISPLAY MODES, specified 2026-08-03 — an enum, not a boolean.** (1) **Off** — the generic starfield alone, as today. (2) **True** — charted systems drawn at their correct direction, apparent magnitude and colour, sitting in front of the backdrop and otherwise indistinguishable from it. (3) **Marked** — the same stars given DIFFRACTION SPIKES so they stand out from the backdrop. **The third mode earns its place for a reason worth keeping:** diffraction spikes are an INSTRUMENT artifact — the cross thrown by a telescope's secondary-mirror supports — not something the naked eye sees. So they read as "this object is ANNOTATED" rather than as a claim about the sky, which is exactly the honest way to say "these dots are your charted systems and the rest is scenery". It also answers the obvious usability question that mode (2) raises on its own: with 42 real systems scattered through a generic starfield, how does anyone tell which is which? **Scale the spikes with brightness** — longer on brighter stars, as real astrophotography does — so the derived magnitude becomes visible rather than merely correct, and a GM can read the important systems at a glance. **Follow the existing preset enum pattern** (the grid types are the model) rather than two booleans, and put it with the other presentation options; note the [[G5]] split applies — a GM-local preference and a preset-carried one are different settings. **The payoff beyond the look: it makes the campaign self-consistent.** The stars a party sees from Tau Ceti become the same stars on the GM's starmap, which is the sort of thing a group notices and remembers. **BUILT v2.1.385-beta — `src/lib/map/skyStars.ts` (the astronomy, pure and tested) plus a far-field renderer in `holo/scene.ts`. It was assembly, exactly as the entry said.** **BOTH ANCHORS LAND, AND SO DOES THE WHOLE REST OF THE SKY.** Sol from Alpha Centauri comes out at **0.48** against the expected 0.5, and from Tau Ceti at **2.64** against 2.6. Then, for free and without a single tunable: seen from Sol the model gives **Sirius -1.58** (real -1.46), **Alpha Centauri -0.28** (-0.27), **Vega 0.25** (0.03), **Procyon 0.46** (0.34) and **Altair 0.82** (0.77) — the five brightest stars of the real night sky, every one inside 0.35 mag, and they are asserted in the spec rather than admired. The only inputs are the map's own positions and each star's `radiationOutput`, so there is nothing to fit. **REUSE HELD, and the one judgement call is worth recording.** Distance goes through `map/systemDistance`, the module every other consumer already uses; luminosity is `radiationOutput`, which IS the star's output in solar units and is what the radiation model reads — not recomputed from radius and temperature, which would have been the second calculation the entry warned about. **Colour comes from `starColorFromTempK` and NOT from `getPlanetColor`**, and that is deliberate: `getPlanetColor` returns a per-CLASS signal swatch picked to be legible on a map and it reads a Svelte store, whereas a star hanging in a sky is showing you its photosphere. No new palette was added. `systemVisualStars` decides which nodes are the stars, so the sky and both starmaps cannot disagree about that. **One point per SYSTEM, not per star** — a pair a few arcseconds apart is a single point to the eye — so the light adds and the colour is the dominant star's; a black hole is skipped, having no photosphere. **FAR-FIELD, as predicted, and that is what made it cheap:** every star is a DIRECTION on a sphere at a fixed radius of 860, just inside the generic starfield's 900. It never touches the floating origin or the rebase. The frame convention is stated in the code — the starmap's plane IS the system's reference plane, physics (x,y,z) to scene (x,z,y) — because there is no other frame to relate the two by, and both maps already treat map-z as height. **THE THREE QUESTIONS, ANSWERED WITH MEASUREMENTS RATHER THAN OPINIONS.** **(1) CULL THE FAINT ONES? Yes in `true`, no in `marked`, and the split is the point.** `true` claims to be a sky, so it stops at the naked-eye limit of 6.5: of 41 systems only 13 are visible from Sol and the faintest is magnitude **19.9**, so without a cut two thirds of what is drawn would be a lie about what anyone standing there could see. `marked` claims to be an INSTRUMENT — that is what a diffraction spike says — so it is not bound by an eye and shows every charted system. **(2) LABELS? Yes, and ONLY in `marked`.** In `true` the whole point is that these are indistinguishable from the backdrop, and a floating name gives the game away. Names belong to the annotated mode, and they reuse the scene's own label sprite so the GPU filter tints them like everything else. **(3) PLAYER VIEW OR GM TOO? Player only, because THE GM HAS NO 3D SYSTEM VIEW AT ALL.** Checked rather than assumed: `HoloView` is mounted by the catalogue, the preset editor's preview and the body graphic, and by nothing in the GM app, whose system view is the 2D orrery. So this is one implementation that lands wherever the 3D scene is, and a GM 3D view would inherit it free. **THE MEASUREMENT THAT SHOULD TEMPER EXPECTATIONS, and it is the reason `marked` opens the cut.** A 45-degree field covers **3.8% of the celestial sphere**. Thirteen naked-eye systems therefore put an EXPECTED HALF A STAR on screen, and in the default framing the real count is **zero** — verified by projecting every star against the live camera. All 41 raise that to about 1.6, and three were on screen with names in the same test. **So on a 42-system map this reads as "there is Sol, over there" rather than as constellations**, and that is a property of the map's density, not of the cut: the real sky has some nine thousand naked-eye stars. It comes alive on a campaign map with hundreds of systems, and the feature is right either way — but nobody should expect to trace a figure out of the bundled Local Neighbourhood. A test pins both halves of that arithmetic so it cannot quietly change. **SPIKES SCALE WITH BRIGHTNESS as asked** — measured 21.7 to 88.9 scene units across the bundled sky, a four-fold range — so the derived magnitude is readable rather than merely correct. Four vanes, tapering to nothing, which is what a real secondary-mirror support throws. **Follows the preset enum pattern** (`constellations: 'off' | 'true' | 'marked'`), optional and defaulting to `off`, so nothing already saved changes; the picker sits with Starfield and only appears when the starfield is on, since these have nowhere to be without it. **VERIFIED BY DRIVING THE REAL SCENE**, not a reproduction: `setSkyStars` emits 0 sprites at `off`, 13 at `true` (radius exactly 860, sizes 3.63 to 12.70 driven by magnitude) and 39 at `marked` (13 dots + 13 spikes + 13 labels), and toggling back to `off` clears them. **What has NOT been seen is the LOOK** — the Browser pane composited briefly and then stopped, so nobody has yet judged whether a spiked star reads as an annotation or as clutter. That is the one thing left to eyeball. **TWEAKED v2.1.401-beta after the first look, and one of the three was a bug rather than a preference.** (1) **"Star boost", 0-100%** — how far the charted stars stand out from the generic starfield. ONE dial, because what is being set is the CONTRAST between two populations: it fades the backdrop (opacity 0.9 down to 0.18) and lifts the charted stars together (dot size 12.7 to 19.7, opacity saturating at 1, spikes 88.9 to 137.8). Pushing only one of them runs out of headroom before they separate, which is why it is not two controls. The top of the range deliberately OVERSATURATES, and past that point it is presentation rather than a claim about apparent magnitude — worth knowing before anyone reads brightness off a boosted sky. (2) **"Name size" in screen px, where 0 is OFF** — as much the point of the control as the sizing is, because with names off you get the diffraction spikes alone and a pattern reads more cleanly. Sizes convert px to world units against the live camera and viewport, so a window resize rebuilds them. Both settings are optional preset data, so nothing already saved changes. (3) **THE LABELS WERE BEING TRUNCATED, and that was a fault in `makeGridLabel` rather than a G9 one.** It drew into a fixed 128x40 canvas sized for "5 AU", so anything longer lost its tail silently — the constellation names measure up to **224px**, so most were cut mid-word. The canvas is measured from the text now, which removes the class of bug instead of widening the guess; grid labels are unchanged on screen (same glyph height, same left anchor, a canvas that fits). **Still not eyeballed:** the pane would not composite for this pass either, so the dials were verified numerically against the live scene and the LOOK of a boosted sky remains unseen. Related: [[A19]], [[G8]], [[G5]]. | built v2.1.385-beta, tweaked v2.1.401-beta |
| G8 | **"Next eclipse, and how big" — a derivable, evocative fact the engine already has every input for.** Proposed 2026-08-02: can a body always say when its next eclipse is, and its size? **Yes. Nothing exists yet — a search for eclipse/occult/umbra machinery returns only CSS `box-shadow` — but every input is present.** The propagator returns positions at ARBITRARY time (`computeWorldPositions3D(system, timeMs)`), radii are authored or derived, and orbital inclinations are now framed correctly after [[C3]]. So "next eclipse" is a forward search over the propagator for an alignment, and "how big" is the ratio of angular diameters, each just `radius / distance`. **THAT RATIO IS THE GOOD PART, because it decides the KIND of eclipse and it is pure arithmetic.** Ratio near 1 gives a total eclipse; occulter smaller than the star gives an annular one; much smaller and it is only a transit — a dot crossing a disc. **Three calibration anchors sit in the bundled Sol and all three are famous, so this can be validated rather than trusted:** Luna and the Sun subtend almost exactly the same angle from Earth, which is WHY totality is possible and why it only just covers; **Phobos is far too small ever to give totality and its real transits are annular**; Deimos is barely more than a moving speck. If the model reproduces those three it is behaving. **DESIGN QUESTIONS TO ANSWER RATHER THAN ASSUME.** (1) **Observed from WHERE?** An eclipse happens somewhere. The natural readings are a satellite crossing the star as seen from its primary, and the primary eclipsing the star as seen from a moon — both are interesting and they are different events. Choose, and say which. (2) **COST: a forward search over a propagator is not free, so it must be ON DEMAND and never inside a derivation pass.** [[B13]] is the cautionary tale — a per-pass cost that also broke idempotence. Compute when a reader asks, cache against the clock, and keep it out of `process()`. (3) **HONESTY ABOUT WHAT IS PREDICTED.** Real eclipse seasons arise from NODAL PRECESSION, which this engine almost certainly does not model — so the answer is "when these elements next align", not an ephemeris. That is perfectly good for a game and it should say so rather than implying observatory precision. **Sequence AFTER [[C5]]**: inclination and reference frame are the whole game here, and predicting eclipses on a frame that is about to change means doing it twice. **BUILT v2.1.383-beta - `src/lib/system/eclipses.ts`, validated against all three anchors and shipped into the shared body block.** **THE THREE DESIGN QUESTIONS, ANSWERED.** **(1) OBSERVED FROM WHERE: you are standing on the body whose data is open, at the point where the shadow falls.** That one viewpoint covers BOTH readings the entry asked about - a satellite crossing the star as seen from its primary, and the primary covering the star as seen from a moon - because both are "the star gets hidden from where I stand", and the number it yields is something an observer actually experiences. A THIRD reading turned up that the entry did not list and it is not a footnote: **barycentre PARTNERS**. Pluto and Charon eclipsed each other every few days through the late eighties and that is how Charon's radius was measured; a member's parent is the BARYCENTRE, so the partner is a sibling rather than a parent or a child, and a rule written for parents and children misses it entirely. **Standing on the SURFACE rather than at the centre is not a refinement either, and getting the surface point wrong was the one real modelling error made here.** The obvious choice is the sub-OCCULTER point, directly under the moon. It is wrong: it pins the observer to whatever latitude the occulter is over, and for a moon in its primary's equatorial plane that is the equator - so Phobos appeared to transit only at the martian equinoxes, when in reality its shadow tracks a wide band of latitudes and rovers see transits through most of the year. The observer stands where the SHADOW falls, on the axis star-occulter-here. The displacement matters at all because it is a whole body radius, which for Luna is 1.7% of the distance - and 1.7% is the difference between an eclipse that just covers the Sun and one that just fails to. **Totality lives in that margin.** **(2) COST: two free pre-filters, a season gate, and a reduced system - and never a derivation pass.** Nothing here is reachable from `process()`; it is called by `bodyFacts` when a reader asks, and `nextEclipseCached` holds the answer until the date it predicted has gone by, which is the whole caching rule and comes straight from how it is read. **Pre-filter one is the 25% floor** - how dark it could EVER get is arithmetic on orbital radii, so a speck is dismissed without a single propagation, which is what stops Deimos at 1%. **Pre-filter two answers "does the shadow ever MISS?"**, and it exists because searching for a date is worst exactly where a date is least meaningful: Io passes into Jupiter's shadow on essentially every one of its 1.77-day orbits, so the honest answer is "every 1.8 d" and the search is skipped entirely. **The test is geometry, NOT "is it a moon of a giant", and Callisto is why:** its miss distance reaches 109,000 km against Jupiter's 69,911, so it escapes the shadow for part of the cycle - exactly what the real Callisto does, alone among the Galileans. **A POLAR orbit falls out of the same arithmetic**: the plane-to-plane angle is near 90 degrees there, the miss distance is the whole orbital radius, and such a moon gets fierce SEASONS instead of a cycle. **The season gate is that same geometry taken per moment** - over one orbit the closest the shadow axis comes to the observing body is `a * sin(beta)`, beta being the star's angular distance from the orbit plane, so a whole non-season is skipped at a coarse stride. An earlier version used a loose margin instead and was useless: it never closed for Phobos at all. **And the biggest win was not an algorithm.** The propagator places EVERY node on every call, so the search is handed a system reduced to the three bodies involved plus their ancestors - the same propagator on a smaller input. **Saturn went from 8.2 s to 243 ms, a 34x cut**; every body in Sol now answers in under 250 ms cold and 0.1 ms cached, 584 ms for all 24 at once, and in practice only the SELECTED body is ever computed. **(3) HONESTY: `approximate` is on every prediction and the wording never implies otherwise.** Elements are held fixed - there is no nodal precession here - so what is answered is "when these elements next line up", not an ephemeris. **THE THREE ANCHORS ALL LAND, and they are asserted as physics rather than pinned to whatever the code printed.** Luna and the Sun come out at a ratio of 1.06 from Earth's surface, which is why the eclipse found is a near-thing 60% partial; **Phobos comes out at 0.607, giving a 37% ANNULAR transit and never a total one** - the real figure is about a third of the disc; Deimos comes out at 0.10, about 1%, below the floor and never propagated. Earth from Luna is 3.7x the Sun, so Luna gets totality. All 23 tests in `eclipses.spec.ts` read the bundled Sol directly rather than a fixture. **PRESENTATION: a date and a percentage, in the SHARED body block** (`bodyFacts`), so it reaches the GM inspector, the printed player report and the 2D/3D info panels from one place - `describeEclipse` builds the string so three surfaces cannot word it three ways. It reads "in 107 d - 37% annular (Phobos)", or "every 1.8 d - total (Jupiter)" for a cycle, or an absolute date when the caller supplies its campaign calendar's formatter. **The row is OMITTED when no clock is passed**, rather than guessed: a caller with no clock has not asked for one. The document surfaces redraw a canvas, so they get the clock at 1 Hz of WALL time rather than per frame - a date only needs to be right to the second, whatever the time scale. **WHAT WAS NOT DONE: no visual check.** The Browser pane does not composite here, so the row was verified by driving `bodyFacts` and `buildGuideDocument` live in the page and reading the strings back, not by looking at it. Someone should open Mars in the GM inspector and in a printed player report and confirm the row sits sensibly among the orbital rows in both. **TWO FINDINGS APPENDED BELOW**, one of them the reason this could not simply reuse the propagator as written: **`computeWorldPositions3D` does NOT apply C3's satellite frame.** Related: [[C3]], [[C5]], [[B13]]. **THE GM PANEL HAS THE ROW TOO, from v2.1.413-beta — owner's request, and it closes the gap this entry's own design left.** `describeEclipse` was written so "the same string reaches the GM panel, the printed player report and the info panels", but only the player-facing surfaces were ever wired; the GM's read-only body block (`BodyTechnicalDetails`) had every other orbital row and not this one. It now sits at the END of the orbital group, after Resonance, which is where it belongs because that is what it is made of. Same builder, same wording, same clock - `currentTime` in `SystemView` is the one already broadcast to the player views, so the two surfaces cannot disagree, and two bodies were checked against the owner's own screenshots and match to the digit (Earth "60% partial (Luna)", Uranus "88% partial (Ariel)"). **The clock is sampled once a second of WALL time, and that is not decoration:** `nextEclipseCached` absorbs a repeated read, but a GM SCRUBBING BACKWARDS lands before the cache entry's `from` on every frame, and every one of those misses is a real search. Sampling on wall time is the rule this feature already landed on for the document surfaces - a date only needs to be right to the second, whatever the time scale - so it is the same rule, not a new one. Verified live: Mars "in 50 d - 37% annular (Phobos)" (the calibration anchor - Phobos can never be total), Luna "in 89 d - 76% partial (Earth)" (the moon's-eye reading), and Mercury correctly shows NO row. | built v2.1.383-beta; GM panel v2.1.413-beta |
| B7 | **A derived tidal lock does not reconcile an authored rotation period, so the block contradicts itself.** Pandora is shown "Rotation: tidally locked" AND "Day length 41.8 h" on a 3-day orbit. `rotation_period_hours: 41.8` is AUTHORED in the map; `tidallyLocked` is absent from the file and DERIVED at load — correctly, a moon at 0.0038 AU from a gas giant despins fast. The two are never reconciled. For a locked body the sidereal day EQUALS the orbital period (here 72 h), and the solar day with respect to its primary is infinite. Decide which wins and make it one number: physics-drives says the derived lock should set the rotation period (and say it did), the alternative is that an authored rotation suppresses the lock — but showing both is not an option. Applies to every locked body with an authored spin, not just Pandora. **Re-confirmed from the display side by the A5 sweep (v2.1.323):** these two rows are the same class of fault as A5 — two figures that look like one quantity, arriving from different stages — but unlike A5 the display cannot pick a winner honestly, because both values are genuinely present on the body and only physics knows which is real. Left here deliberately rather than papered over in `bodyFacts`. Shares its shape with [[A5]] and [[B6]]. **FIXED v2.1.333-beta.** **Root cause: two stages answering one question, and the temperature model had already picked a side.** `surfaceTemperature.ts:83` reads `effRotHours = tidallyLocked ? (orbitalPeriodHours || ...) : rotationHours` — so the thermal model has ALWAYS used the orbital period for a locked body and ignored the stored spin. The stat block, `rotationFactor()` in the dynamo and `rotationalDeform()` all read the stored number. The lock now sets it, which unifies them rather than adding a third opinion. **MERCURY IS THE ANCHOR AND IT NEARLY BROKE.** A blanket 1:1 rule would have overwritten Mercury's **measured 1407.6 h day** with its 2110.9 h year. Mercury is despun but NOT synchronous: at e = 0.206 it is captured in a 3:2 spin-orbit resonance (Goldreich & Peale 1966). So `lockedSpin()` keeps a spin sitting on a half-integer ratio of the orbit when the orbit is eccentric enough to hold it (e >= 0.1, ratio >= 1.5, within 2% of p:2, order <= 4:1), publishes `orbit/spin-orbit-resonance` = "3:2", and the stat block prints the resonance instead of "tidally locked". Everything else takes its orbital period. Pinned in the baseline as a value that must NOT move. **What the thresholds RULE OUT, checked body by body rather than assumed:** Chazon (ratio 1.523, within 1.5% of 3:2) is excluded by e = 0.029 — too circular to hold it; Conquestor (ratio 0.508, e = 0.126) by the sub-synchronous floor, since despinning passes THROUGH 1:2 toward 1:1 and does not stop there; Fames (e = 0.29), Adonis, Bellum and Fuller by not being near any half-integer. Across all 561 bundled bodies the exception fires on exactly one body — Mercury, four times, because Sol appears in four maps. **The numbers, measured against a worktree at the parent commit.** 23 locked bodies had a spin contradicting their own orbit; **23 -> 4, and all 4 are Mercury**. 117 locked bodies had NO spin at all and now have one. In Sol 20 rotation periods moved and **every one by under 1.2%** — their authored day already WAS their orbital period, so the shift is only our Kepler solution against the real one: Pluto/Charon 1.12%, Deimos/Ariel 0.72%, Luna 655.7 -> 659.03 h (0.51%), Io 0.02%. Pluto's retrograde sign survives. In the fictional maps the moves are large because the numbers were flavour: **Pandora 41.8 -> 74.76 h** (the reported body), LV-426 50 -> 80.6, Adonis 15 -> 2630.8, Bellum 9.05 -> 2152.3. **No temperature, no habitability and NO CLASSIFICATION moved anywhere** — expected, since the thermal model was already using the orbital period. **The one consequence worth arguing about, stated plainly: 53 bodies lost `magnetic/dynamo`** (41 to unshielded, 12 to tenuous), including Proxima b, Ross 128 b, Luyten b and the outer TRAPPIST-1 worlds. **40 of them had no rotation at all**, so `rotationFactor`'s "unknown -> 0.6" default had been handing every rotation-less planet an Earth-like field — a magnetosphere derived from not knowing, the same fault class as [[B9]]. Given their real 9-49 day spin the model says what it has always said about a slow rotator. **The threshold was NOT retuned to soften this** — see [[B12]]: it has no anchor between Venus and Earth and is now load-bearing for ~40 bundled planets. One body also lost `shape/oblate`, correctly: a world forced to a 200 h day is not flattened. Also found while measuring: [[B13]]. | fixed v2.1.333-beta |
| B5 | Mars's surface albedo is 0.154 from rock + metal makeup alone; the measured Bond albedo is 0.25, because Mars is bright with ferric dust and polar caps. Exposed by B1: the bogus CO2 deck had been carrying Mars to 0.236, right for the wrong reason. Costs Mars its real 210 ppm water-ice wisp (it survives to Teq ≈ 214.5 K; Mars now sits at 216.7). Same class: Io 0.153 vs measured 0.63 (SO2 frost), Luna 0.15 vs 0.11, Mercury 0.169 vs 0.088. `deriveOxidation()` already grades the rust but reads `geoActivity.surfaceAgeGyr`, derived AFTER the thermal solve — so this needs the geology derivation moved too, or it reintroduces the one-pass lag v2.1.282 removed. **Plan when it gets a session:** it is a surface-albedo model, not a Mars patch — the measurements point three different ways at once. Bare rock wants to be DARKER than the current flat 0.15 (Mercury 0.088, Luna 0.11 — the model is too bright for both), while bodies with surface DEPOSITS want to be brighter (Mars 0.25 from ferric dust, Io 0.63 from SO2 frost). So: lower the bare-rock constant, then add brightening from the deposits the engine already derives — `surface/oxidised` for rust, frost/cap coverage for the rest. The blocker is ordering: everything that grades those deposits (`deriveOxidation`, `deriveGeoActivity`) runs after the thermal solve, and `deriveGeoActivity` itself takes `teqK`, so it cannot simply move — either the non-thermal part of geology splits out ahead of the solve, or surface deposits join the fixed point. Expect real classification movement; needs the same before/after diff B1 used. **DECIDED 2026-08-02: ATTEMPT IT, KEEPING THE GATE.** The ordering hazard has not changed and the gate stands exactly as written — if geology cannot be separated from `teqK` without creating a new circular read, STOP and write up rather than reintroducing the one-pass lag v2.1.282 removed. **What HAS changed is that the pattern is now proven:** [[B13]] moved a derivation across a pass boundary and pinned it with an idempotence test, which is precisely what this needs; note B13's own caveat that its move was DOWN across the boundary and this one is UP across the thermal solve, which is the harder direction. Take B13's idempotence test as the model and ship one with the fix. **A CONCRETE, QUANTIFIED ACCEPTANCE TEST, added 2026-08-01 after "Mars once had CO2 wisps of cloud and they are not visible now".** Confirmed in `tests/output/solar-system-derived.json`: Mars carries `teqK = 216.7116` and **no `structure/cloud-deck` tag at all**, while Venus (sulfuric-acid veil), Earth (water overcast) and Titan (methane overcast) each carry one. The threshold recorded above is Teq 214.5 K, so **the deck is missing by 2.2 K** — and the missing deck is the WATER-ice wisp at 210 ppm (`H2O: 0.00021` in the campaign data), not a CO2 one; Mars has real CO2 clouds but they were never what the engine modelled. Since `Teq` goes as `(1 - A)^(1/4)`, the arithmetic is direct: at the model's A = 0.154 Mars sits at 216.7 K, and at the measured Bond albedo A = 0.25 it would sit at **210.2 K**, i.e. 4.3 K BELOW the survival threshold, so the deck returns with margin rather than marginally. **That is the acceptance test for this item: fix the albedo model and Mars regains its wisp WITHOUT a single line of cloud code or any Mars-specific branch.** If the wisp does not come back, the albedo change has not moved far enough; if it comes back on a body that should not have one, the deposit brightening is too generous. **Note the history, because it explains why this looked like a regression:** the wisp used to appear, propped up by the bogus CO2 deck B1 deleted, which had been carrying Mars to A = 0.236 — very close to the real 0.25, but right for the wrong reason. Nothing broke; the fault was always there and B1 removed what was concealing it. **NOT BUILT. STOPPED AT THE HARD GATE, deliberately, with the evidence below. B5 STAYS OPEN.** **The clean split does not exist, and the triage above undercounts the problem by three.** It says `deriveGeoActivity` "itself takes `teqK`, so it cannot simply move". True, but `teqK` is one of **four** inputs that are downstream of the thermal solve, and it is the least of them: 1. `teqK` — direct, but used in exactly ONE branch (`geoActivity.ts:141`, the Triton solar-seasonal geyser, `teqK < 60`). On its own this one WOULD be carvable. 2. `hasSurfaceWater` — from `deriveFluidLayers` at `SystemProcessor.ts:657`, pass THREE. The solve is at `:416-433`, pass two. 3. `hasSubsurfaceOcean` — same `fluidLayers` call, same pass. 4. `icyShell` at `:684` — from `surfacePhase` and `makeupIceIsSolid`, and **[[B4]] added that temperature gate deliberately at v2.1.283** ("ice-rich interior and icy shell are different claims"). So carving out the single `teqK` branch would not free geology: the water-PHASE inputs would have to come with it, and those are the thermal solve's downstream products by design. Undoing that to make albedo work would undo B4. **What "deposits join the fixed point" would actually mean, since that is the remaining route.** Every iteration of `solveThermalState` would have to run fluid layers -> icy shell -> subsurface ocean -> `deriveGeoActivity` -> `deriveSurfaceAgeGyr` -> `deriveOxidation` -> surface albedo. The solve is deliberately NON-MUTATING — evaluated against a probe so the answer never depends on what a previous `process()` left on the body — and all seven of those derivations mutate the body today. That is a rewrite of most of pass 3, not an ordering tweak, and getting it wrong reintroduces exactly the one-pass lag v2.1.282 removed. Which is not hypothetical: [[B13]] is that lag, alive right now, in surface radiation. **THE OBVIOUS SHORTCUT IS THE TRAP, and it is worth writing down before someone reaches for it.** "Do the frost half and leave the rust" looks free — frost and cap coverage need no geology. It is the single most dangerous version: **a BRIGHT condensate makes the solve BISTABLE**, and frost is the canonical case (ice-albedo feedback is genuinely bistable in nature — that is what snowball Earth IS). The rule from the albedo session applies at full strength. **And the bare-rock half ALONE would make Mars worse.** Current anchors, read from the derived output: Mercury 0.169 (measured 0.088), Luna 0.150 (0.11), **Mars 0.154 (0.25)**, Io 0.153 (0.63). Lowering the bare-rock constant toward Luna and Mercury takes Mars to about 0.11 — further from its measurement, warmer, with knock-on into its cloud decks and every rocky body in every map. Fixing two anchors by breaking a third is what [[B2]]'s rule exists to prevent. The two halves have to land together, exactly as the plan says. **Two things established that make the next attempt cheaper.** (a) **Mars ALREADY carries `surface/oxidised=moderate`** — the rust signal exists and is graded; nothing needs deriving, it is purely a question of when. Venus, TRAPPIST-1 c/d, Adrian, Chazon and 11 others carry it too: **17 bodies of 561**, and `climate/polar-ice` covers 10. Io carries no frost tag at all, so its 0.63 needs a NEW SO2-frost derivation rather than a wire-up — the Io case is not "read the tag". (b) **`SURF_ALBEDO = { metal: 0.18, rock: 0.15, carbon: 0.05, ice: 0.62, gas: 0.30 }` is hardcoded at `albedo.ts:22`**, and B5's own plan requires those in rule-pack DATA per the architecture rule. **RECOMMENDED FIRST STEP for whoever picks this up, and it is safe on its own:** migrate `SURF_ALBEDO` and `OCEAN_ALBEDO` into the rule pack as a pure no-op (same numbers, read from data, empty derived diff). That is independent of the ordering problem, satisfies the constants-in-data rule, and leaves B5 with only the ordering question. NOT done here because the session that builds the model should choose the schema — it will need per-deposit brightening coefficients too, not just the three base numbers, and guessing that shape now means redoing it. **Also weigh the sequencing:** the whole rust half moves 17 bodies, and the price is restructuring the processor's central solve. [[B13]] is the same ordering fault in a cheaper place and is probably the right thing to fix first — it would establish the pattern for moving a derivation across the solve boundary, with an idempotence test, on a subsystem where getting it wrong costs far less. **MEASURED 2026-08-03 (stage 1 of the second attempt), and the measurement CLEARS THE GATE — by contradicting two of the three things the stop above was based on.** **(1) `teqK` IS EFFECTIVELY INERT: perturbing it ±10 K moves `surfaceAgeGyr` on TWO bodies out of 366.** The stop says "`deriveGeoActivity` itself takes `teqK`, so it cannot simply move", and the gate is written around dragging `teqK` backwards. It is the Triton solar-seasonal geyser branch (`teqK < 60`) and nothing else, exactly as the earlier note guessed — but the magnitude was never measured, and it is two bodies. **The hazard the gate names is not the hazard that exists.** **(2) THE REAL COUPLING IS `hasSurfaceWater`, ALONE, AND IT IS TOTAL: flipping it moves `surfaceAgeGyr` on 136 bodies and changes the OXIDATION GRADE on all 136 of them.** (`hasSubsurfaceOcean` 16, `icyShell` 20.) The mechanism is the plate-tectonics branch: surface water → mobile lid → 0.2 Gyr resurfacing → under `deriveOxidation`'s 0.3 Gyr floor → no rust. Earth is the clean case and it is CORRECT: Earth's surface is too young to rust. So the ordering problem is real, but it is one input, not four. **(3) AND THAT ONE INPUT IS ALREADY COMPUTED INSIDE THE SOLVE.** `deriveAlbedo` (`albedo.ts`, the hydrosphere branch) already calls `phaseAtP(hydroComp, surfT, pressure)` on the probe every iteration to decide whether the surface liquid is dark ocean or bright frost. "Is there liquid water standing on the surface" is that same question. The solve is not missing the information; nothing is currently passing it to geology. **(4) THE "REWRITE OF MOST OF PASS 3" IS NOT REQUIRED, because the claim that "all seven of those derivations mutate the body" is wrong for the three that matter.** `deriveFluidLayers(body, pack)` returns `FluidLayer[]`; `deriveGeoActivity(i: GeoInputs)` returns a `GeoActivity`; `deriveOxidation(body)` returns a string. **All three are pure functions** — the PROCESSOR assigns their results, the functions do not mutate. `solveThermalState`'s `evaluate()` already calls two pure derivations (`deriveCloudDecks`, `deriveAlbedo`) against a shallow `probe` for precisely this reason, and its own comment says so: "Neither writes, so a shallow copy is enough." Adding three more pure calls to that probe is the same pattern, not a new one. **Nothing moves backwards: geology is still COMMITTED in pass 3 from the converged temperature, and gets the same answer because it is the same pure function on the same converged inputs — which is what makes it idempotent rather than lagged.** **(5) THE BISTABILITY THE STOP WARNS ABOUT IS REAL BUT IS ONE BODY.** The feedback is genuine — colder → water freezes → not "surface water" → stagnant lid → older surface → more rust → brighter → colder — and it is the snowball-Earth loop, so it must be bounded, not dismissed. Measured: 34 bundled bodies are rust-eligible (iron ≥ 0.05 and an oxidiser), and exactly **ONE** of those sits within 8 K of the water freezing point with a hydrosphere — Testion's "forest" (T 268.9 K, coverage 0.4, metal 0.19, oxidiser 0.63). That is a containable, nameable risk with a convergence assertion, not an unbounded one. **CURRENT ANCHORS, re-read rather than trusted:** Mercury surf/bond 0.169 (measured 0.088), Luna 0.150 (0.11), **Mars 0.154 (0.25), teq 216.7 K, `surface/oxidised=moderate`, `surfaceAgeGyr` 3.85, regime inactive, NO cloud deck**, Io 0.153 (0.63), teq 117.2, no frost tag of any kind. All four cloud-deck-free, so the anchors are pure surface albedo with nothing overhead confusing them. **BUILT v2.1.372-beta. ALL FOUR ANCHORS LAND FROM ONE MODEL WITH NO PER-WORLD BRANCH — Mercury 0.169→0.088 (measured 0.088), Luna 0.150→0.110 (0.11), Mars 0.154→0.256 (0.25), Io 0.153→0.569 (0.63)** — and **the acceptance test passes on its own predicted number: Mars goes to teq 209.8 K against the 210.2 this entry forecast, and its 210 ppm WATER-ice wisp condenses again** (`structure/cloud-deck=water wisps` plus its virga), with no cloud code touched and no Mars-specific branch anywhere. **THE MODEL.** Bare ground is darker than the flat 0.15, and **metal is darker than rock** — a space-weathered iron regolith is about the darkest natural surface there is, which is why Mercury at 62% metal is the darkest rocky body in the Solar System. Brightness is then ADDED by two deposits the engine already derives: oxide dust from `surface/oxidised`, and **volatile frost wherever the atmosphere's dominant gas is below ITS OWN freezing point**. The second is deliberately general rather than an Io rule, and generality is what makes the split come out right on its own: Io's SO2 freezes at 198 K and Io sits near 100, so Io is FROST; Mars at 210 K is above CO2's 195 K, so Mars is DUST and not frost — exactly the two different mechanisms this entry said the measurements were pointing at. **HOW THE GATE WAS HONOURED.** Nothing moved backwards across the solve. The rust is EVALUATED INSIDE the fixed point against the same non-mutating probe `deriveCloudDecks` and `deriveAlbedo` already use; the processor still COMMITS geology in its own pass from the converged temperature and gets the same answer back, which is what makes it idempotent rather than one pass behind. [[B13]]'s idempotence test — Sol, imported Sol, both starmaps, every example — passes unchanged, and that is the proof. The system age is threaded into `solveThermalState` rather than defaulted, because it must be the same figure the processor uses when it commits geology or the albedo would be computed against a different surface age than the body ends up carrying. **THE BISTABILITY WAS BOUNDED, NOT DISMISSED: 0 of 366 bodies fail to converge, before or after, and the acceptance test asserts it.** **EVERY MOVER JUDGED.** Albedo moves on 254 of 366, mean 0.036. **Class changes: ONE distinct body** — Proxima Centauri d sub-earth→hot-eyeball, because a darker surface takes it 317.1→322.7 K and over the 320 floor: **BETTER**, its real equilibrium temperature is nearer 360 and it is tidally locked, hot and dry. **THE ONE THING THAT GOT WORSE, reported rather than buried: PLUTO LOSES its methane wisps.** Its nitrogen frosts out, taking it 0.299→0.579 against a measured ~0.72 — a large gain — and the resulting 36 K surface then drops the methane deck. Real Pluto does have methane haze, so **the cloud-deck model may want a look at very low temperatures**; not fixed here. LV-426's ammonia deck thins from overcast to veil. Everything else holds: Earth 0.308→0.303, Venus 0.757 unmoved, Titan 0.285→0.278, Europa 0.62 unmoved, Triton 0.618 unmoved and keeps its deck. **Derived diff: ZERO class changes in Sol and exactly TWO tag changes** — Mars gains the wisp pair, Pluto loses the methane pair; the rest of the 950 lines is albedo and the temperatures that follow it. **Constants are rule-pack DATA** (`planets.json` `surface_albedo`), per this entry's own recommended first step — including the per-grade dust coverage and the frost ramp, not only the three base numbers, so the schema question it flagged is answered. **Three corrections to this entry, all from stage 1's measurement:** `teqK` is not the blocker (±10 K moves TWO bodies); `hasSurfaceWater` is the entire coupling (136 bodies, all changing oxidation grade); and "all seven of those derivations mutate the body" is wrong for the three that matter — `deriveFluidLayers`, `deriveGeoActivity` and `deriveOxidation` are pure, which is what made the whole thing a three-line addition to `evaluate()` rather than the rewrite of pass 3 this entry feared. | fixed v2.1.372-beta |
| B3 | Classification keys on EQUILIBRIUM temperature, not surface — a 309 °C runaway greenhouse can be labelled "cold eyeball". The eyeball family is fixed: all three fingerprints describe the GROUND ("molten/dry dayside", "icy except the substellar point", "temperate oasis") and now match `SurfaceTemp_K`. Pinned by a test; no bundled body changed, because every star-locked world in the shipped maps is airless and its two temperatures agree. | fixed v2.1.283-beta |
| B6 | The REST of B3. 26 more fingerprints key on `Teq_K`, and they split cleanly in two. Types whose note describes the surface should follow the eyeballs to `SurfaceTemp_K`: desert, ice ("Frozen surface"), lava ("Molten surface"), ocean, methane, ammonia-planet, hycean, subsurface-ocean, earth-analogue, earth-like, superhabitable, forest, jungle, swamp. Types about the RADIATION ENVIRONMENT or a giant's cloud-top chemistry are right as they are: hot-jupiter, ultra-hot-jupiter, hot-neptune, ultra-hot-neptune, chthonian, ice-giant, ultra-cool-dwarf, and all five gas-giant cloud classes. Evidence the bands were authored as surface temperatures in the first place: earth-analogue wants Teq 255–300 and Earth's Teq is 254.1, just outside — its SURFACE is 287, mid-band. Not done here because it is ~14 fingerprints and would move real classifications; wants its own pass with a before/after diff. **Noted by the A5 sweep (v2.1.323) as VISIBLE, not just internal:** the stat block prints Type and Surface temp two rows apart, so a fingerprint keyed on `Teq_K` puts a contradiction on the page — the same class of fault as A5, and the reason this is worth more than a classifier tidy-up. **FIXED v2.1.335-beta, exactly as scoped — 14 moved, 12 left, no argument with the split.** **FIVE bodies change class across all 561 in every bundled system, and all five are in `Testion-System.json`, the one-body-per-class fixture. Judged individually, as asked:** - **ammonia planet -> desert. BETTER.** Teq 187 K but SURFACE 346 K. Ammonia boils at ~250 K at its authored 1.5 bar, so the body had no ammonia hydrosphere to be named after. Note its fingerprint even carries a `range` of Teq 120-220 which it satisfied — the surface was 126 K outside. - **earth like -> terrestrial. BETTER.** Surface 345.8 K, i.e. 73 °C, labelled "Habitable Earth-like world". Its authored air is 1% CO2, 25x Earth's, which is where the +92 K comes from — the body really is a hothouse and was only ever earth-like by reading the wrong temperature. - **superhabitable -> super-earth. BETTER.** Surface 371.4 K, 98 °C, labelled "even better for life than Earth", with 80% water coverage that would be at boiling. Correctly drops `science/rare-world-type` with it. - **swamp -> jungle. NEUTRAL, and say so rather than claim a win.** Surface 298.1 K is inside BOTH bands (swamp 278-300, jungle 290-318), both weigh 1.5, and the margin is 0.04 points. A tie-break flip, not an accuracy change. - **coreless -> desert. LATERAL, arguably a small loss, and the cause is NOT the temperature key.** A dry 268 K rock: "desert" is true of its surface and "coreless" is true of its interior, but both are `kind: base` and bases are mutually exclusive, so only one survives. B6 merely tipped a scoring contest that base-exclusivity had already set up. **Why the blast radius is small, measured rather than assumed:** only **131 of 366** bundled planets/moons opt into `autoClassify` at all, and of those the ones with a real greenhouse mostly carry AUTHORED classes that the processor must not overwrite. 88 of 366 bodies have a greenhouse over 10 K and 59 over 50 K, but most of that population never reaches the classifier. **THE ITEM'S HEADLINE EVIDENCE IS TRUE BUT WAS NOT LOAD-BEARING, and the next person should know.** Earth's Teq 254.1 does sit just outside earth-analogue's 255-300 — but `bandFit` decays LINEARLY over a 15% relative margin instead of cutting off, so 254.1 scored 0.977 and Earth classified anyway. What actually changed is the strength of the fit: Earth's best-scoring type now scores a clean 1.0 on every band (1.8 -> 2.1). That is still the right evidence for the split, just not for the reason recorded. **ONE REGRESSION FOUND AND FIXED IN THE SAME COMMIT, caught by the existing tests.** `viableTypesAt` — the generator's "what could exist at this orbit" menu — read `fp.match['Teq_K']` only, and treats a type with no such band as having NO temperature constraint. So every moved type became viable everywhere and lava was offered in the Goldilocks zone. It reads either key now. **The same regression has been live for the three EYEBALL classes since v2.1.283, when [[B3]] moved them, with nothing covering it** — fixed here and now pinned. The menu picks a type for a body that does not exist yet and so cannot have a surface temperature; `GREENHOUSE_COLD_SLACK_K` already exists to bridge exactly that gap, which is independent evidence the bands were authored as surface bands all along. **Sol: no body changes class.** Seven classification EXPLANATIONS change, which is the score improving. Also raised: [[B14]], [[B15]]. | fixed v2.1.335-beta |
| B4 | The ice-shell rule (ices float → visible crust) has no temperature gate; it will paint an ice crust on a 582 K world. The makeup branch (`mk.ice > 0.3`) now also requires water to be SOLID at the surface T and P. Ice-rich interior and icy shell are different claims. Three bundled bodies lost a false `structure/icy-shell` (Lacaille 9352 c at 303 K, GJ 674 b at 488 K, GJ 876 d at 523 K); nothing else moved. | fixed v2.1.283-beta |
| B8 | **The surface-radiation mean can sit marginally OUTSIDE its own min/max, because the three figures are composed two different ways.** Found by the A5 sweep (v2.1.323) while checking every paired figure in the stat block for A5's fault; recorded rather than fixed, because it is physics and because it is currently invisible. `calculateSurfaceRadiation` (`physics/radiation.ts`) builds `surfaceRadiation` from the PER-STAR spectral components (`components.photon` / `components.particle`, each already shielded), while `fluxToDose` at `:198-202` rebuilds the endpoints from a single flat `rawPhotonFrac`/`rawParticleFrac` applied to `calculateTotalStellarRadiationRange`'s totals. The comment at `:194` says the endpoints are kept "consistent with the same component model used above" — they are consistent in SHAPE but not identical in composition, so the two can disagree in the last fraction of a percent. **Measured on the bundled Sol map: 3 of 34 bodies land outside** — Neptune (2.28 vs a 2.26–2.28 range), Triton (2.57 vs 2.54–2.56), Neptune's Rings (0.57 vs 0.54–0.56). **Worst excess ~0.4%, and the stat block prints the range at one decimal place, so nothing contradictory reaches a reader today** — which is exactly why it is here and not fixed: the display is honest, the model is very slightly not. **Why it is worth a line anyway:** the same fault at a larger amplitude is what A5 was, and a multi-star system (where the spectral mix genuinely differs between the near and far ends of an orbit) is where this would grow rather than stay at 0.4%. Whoever takes it should check a binary before deciding it is negligible. Related: [[A5]]. **FIXED v2.1.334-beta — and BOTH halves of the triage above were wrong, which is the finding.** **The recorded cause is impossible.** The mean is `(photonFlux + particleFlux) * DOSE + bg`, and expanding it gives **exactly** `fluxToDose(components.total)` — the shielded mix cancels algebraically, because `rawPhotonFrac` is defined as `components.photon / total`. `fluxToDose` is linear and monotonic in flux. So the spectral composition CANNOT push the mean outside its own endpoints; the excursion had to be in the flux, one level down. **Real root cause: a missing TERM, not a rounding difference.** `calculateStellarRadiationComponents` adds an episodic flare dose to the particle channel (`star.flareActivity * FLARE_PARTICLE_WEIGHT`); `calculateTotalStellarRadiationRange` was a SECOND sum of the same quantity with no flare term in it at all. The mean therefore carried a dose the endpoints did not, by exactly `(1 + flare/2)` — which is why the excess is systematically ABOVE the maximum and never below the minimum. **The measurement, over all 420 ranged bodies in every bundled system rather than Sol alone: 105 land outside, not 3, and the worst is 19.75% of the mean, not 0.4%.** Wolf 359's Graveyard reads 4.46 against a 3.30-3.58 range — Wolf 359 being a famously active flare star, which is the entire mechanism visible in one row. Epsilon Eridani's belts 14.5%, Lacaille 9352 e 5.9%. Sol was simply the benign case: a quiet G star has little flare activity, so its worst body was 0.4%. **THE BINARY TEST THE ITEM ASKED FOR REFUTES ITS OWN HYPOTHESIS.** Multi-star systems are the MILDER case — 34 offenders, worst 5.71% — and single-star systems the worse, 71 offenders, worst 19.75%. What drives this is stellar ACTIVITY, not the spectral mix differing between the near and far ends of an orbit. Had it only been checked on a binary it would have been closed as negligible on the wrong evidence. **Fix: delete the second sum.** `calculateStellarRadiationComponents` takes a distance selector, and mean / min / max are all it, evaluated at the current, far and near distances. Every star's split and flare weight are constants of the star and flux falls monotonically with distance, so **min <= mean <= max now holds by construction**, not by luck. 105 -> 0. Pinned by a test whose subject is a flaring star on an eccentric orbit. **Derived diff touches exactly four fields on 68 bodies** — `stellarRadiationMin/Max`, `surfaceRadiationMin/Max`. No mean, no temperature, no habitability, no class moved, which is the expected shape: the mean was always right and the endpoints were short. Sol's three named offenders now bracket — Neptune 2.277 in 2.271-2.284, Triton 2.566 in 2.553-2.579, Neptune's Rings 0.566 in 0.553-0.579. | fixed v2.1.334-beta |

## C — Rendering / appearance

| # | Observation | Status |
|---|---|---|
| C1 | `condensateTint` (cloudDecks.ts ~165) pulls every deck to within 60/255 of white. Right for scattering droplets, wrong for a pigmented suspension — the ceiling is pastel for ANY deck, whatever the data says. Now `LiquidDef.cloudTintDistance`, rule-pack data with the old 60 as the default, so nothing shipped changes look unless its data says so. The physics is that a clean SCATTERER goes white however dark the liquid (water) while an ABSORBING suspension keeps its colour however finely divided (Jupiter's brown hydrosulphide, martian dust). Set on the five genuinely pigmented condensates. **No visible change on the bundled maps** — the only bodies carrying a pigmented deck are giants, and giants take their look from gas chemistry rather than the deck path (E6). It bites the moment someone authors one, or when giants join the deck stack. | fixed v2.1.283-beta |
| C5 | **Which plane does a moon actually orbit in? The Laplace plane, and we approximate it with a flag.** Raised by the C3 audit. C3 rotates a satellite's orbit into its parent's equator, which is right for regular satellites and wrong for distant ones: beyond roughly fifty host radii solar perturbation wins over the parent's oblateness and the orbit follows the SYSTEM plane instead. The real answer is the Laplace plane, which transitions smoothly between the two with distance. C3 approximates it with a per-orbit `frame: 'ecliptic'` declaration, set on Luna (60 Earth radii) and Phoebe (222 Saturn radii). **The case that shows the limit is Iapetus:** at 61 Saturn radii it is squarely in the transition, its orbit genuinely precesses about the Laplace plane, and its quoted 15.47 deg is conventionally referenced to Saturn's equator — so it is currently treated as equatorial and drawn 15.47 deg off the ring plane, which is defensible but not exactly right. **What a proper fix looks like:** compute the Laplace plane from the host's J2, mass ratio and the moon's semi-major axis, and orient the orbit to it — a physics change, in the propagator or a derivation feeding it, needing a before/after across the bundled maps. Until then the flag is the honest lever, and PROC GEN should set it for anything it generates beyond the transition. Related: C3 (c), also open. **MEASURED 2026-08-02, NOT BUILT — and the measurement says do not build it. VERDICT: the flag is not merely a good enough approximation for the bundled data, it is EXACT, and exactly one body is anywhere near the transition.** **Method:** classical Laplace radius `r_L^5 = 2 J2 R_p^2 a_p^3 (M_p/M_star)` and local Laplace-plane inclination to the host's equator `tan(2 i_L) = sin(2 eps) / ((r_L/a)^5 + cos(2 eps))` (Tremaine, Touma & Namouni 2009), computed for all 19 moons in both maps. J2 came from literature — the map carries no J2 field, which is itself worth knowing if this is ever built. **The transition population, by `a/r_L`:** Iapetus **1.44**, then Callisto 0.82, Deimos 0.53, Titan 0.49, Ganymede 0.46, Oberon 0.43, and everything else at or under 0.32. Above the line: Luna 6.25 and Phoebe 5.23, both already flagged. **So the distribution is not a spectrum with several awkward cases — it is a tight equatorial cluster, two bodies out in the ecliptic regime, and Iapetus on its own in the middle.** And the near-misses do not matter even where they are close: Callisto is at 0.82 but Jupiter's obliquity is 3.13 deg, so the ENTIRE equator-to-ecliptic gap it could be wrong by is 3.13 deg and its Laplace plane sits at 0.84; Deimos at 0.53 gives 0.87 deg. Nothing between Iapetus and the cluster is wrong by as much as a degree. **THE METRIC IN THE ORIGINAL ENTRY IS THE WRONG ONE, and getting this right reverses the conclusion about Iapetus.** The Laplace-plane offset is not the rendering error, because a moon's quoted `i_deg` is not referenced to its Laplace plane — it is referenced to the parent's equator (regular satellites) or the ecliptic (Luna, Phoebe). The only question the flag has to answer is WHICH OF THOSE TWO the catalogue number was quoted in, and on that test all 19 are right, including Iapetus: its 15.47 deg is equator-referenced, so equatorial renders it where the real Iapetus is. **C3 left Iapetus alone and calling it "defensible but not exactly right" undersold it — leaving it alone was correct, and setting the flag would have moved it about 7 deg WRONG.** **What the Laplace plane genuinely buys for Iapetus, stated so nobody has to re-derive it:** its orbit precesses about a plane 23.3 deg from Saturn's equator with a free inclination of about 7.9 deg, so its inclination to the equator swings roughly 15.5-31 deg over a precession cycle — order 5,000 years on a rough J2-plus-solar estimate (published figures are nearer 3,000). A static plane is a snapshot that is correct now and drifts over millennia, which is below the resolution of any campaign. **RECOMMENDATION: do not build a continuous Laplace model on the strength of the bundled data — zero bodies would move today.** Its value is entirely in **C3 part (c), PROC GEN**, where there is no catalogue to quote and the generator must CHOOSE a plane rather than record one; there, `a/r_L` is the right switch and the formula above is the whole model. That makes this item a dependency of the generation work, not of the map data. **The renderer was re-verified after the [[D4d]] data changes** by reproducing `scene.ts`'s `toParentEquator` exactly and measuring each moon's orbit normal against the ring-plane normal: Saturn's inner six sit 0.01-1.57 deg from the ring plane, Iapetus is the odd one out at 15.47, Luna 23.97 and Triton 22.70 (= 180 - 157.3, the acute angle for a retrograde orbit). Identical to C3's own figures, so nothing drifted. **What was NOT done: the visual check.** The Browser pane is not displayed in this environment and screenshots time out, so the numbers above are a faithful reproduction of the renderer's transform rather than a look at the screen. Someone should still eyeball Saturn once. **No data changed, so the D4d kit test stays green trivially.** | measured, recommend not building |
| C3 | **[SCOPE WIDENED on request: also AUDIT every moon in both bundled maps, and make PROC GEN incline moons and rings with their host by default.]** **Moon orbits ignore the parent's axial tilt; rings do not — so coplanar bodies are drawn ~27° apart.** Noticed as "Saturn's moons don't sit in the ring plane". The DATA is correct and real: rings `i_deg = 0`, Mimas 1.57, Enceladus 0.009, Dione 0.019, Rhea 0.345, Titan 0.348, Iapetus 15.47 — all quoted the conventional way for regular satellites, i.e. **relative to the parent's equator**. Both ring builders in `holo/scene.ts` (~2584 particle ring, ~2670 band) explicitly rotate into the equatorial plane — "Ring plane = planet equator" — by `parent.axial_tilt_deg`. Moon ORBITS do not get that rotation, so their `i_deg` is applied in the system plane and they stay flat while the rings tilt 26.73°. Real Saturn has its inner moons IN the ring plane, so the picture is misleading; Iapetus should be the only visibly inclined one and is currently wrong the other way. **Fix:** a satellite's orbital reference frame should be the parent's equator, the same rotation the rings already use. **Scope:** every moon system — Jupiter's Galileans, Uranus (98° tilt, so the error is dramatic there), Neptune/Triton. **Confirm the convention first, do not assume:** satellite inclinations are parent-equatorial but PLANET inclinations are ecliptic-relative, so this must apply to satellites only, not to every orbit.  **FIXED (a)+(b) v2.1.316-beta; (c) NOT done.** **The convention held, with two real exceptions — and they are physics, not data errors.** Audited all 31 distinct moons across both bundled maps, 19 of them with a tilted host. Every regular satellite is quoted parent-equatorially as assumed: Saturn's inner six, Jupiter's Galileans, Mars's pair, Uranus's three, Triton. **The exceptions are the far-out ones, and there is a reason:** beyond roughly fifty host radii the LAPLACE PLANE hands over from the parent's equator to the system plane, so a distant moon's orbit follows the ecliptic instead. Luna sits at 60 Earth radii and its 5.145 deg is the ECLIPTIC value (to Earth's equator it is 18.3-28.6 deg and time-varying, so there is no single number to author); Phoebe at 222 Saturn radii is the same story. Both are now declared `orbit.frame: 'ecliptic'` and are left in their own plane — a declaration, in the spirit of this batch, rather than a threshold invented in code. **Iapetus is genuinely ambiguous and was left alone:** 61 Saturn radii puts it squarely in the transition, and its 15.47 deg is usually quoted to Saturn's equator, so it is treated as equatorial. See C5. **Verified numerically rather than by eye, which for angles is the stronger check** — the angle between each moon's orbit normal and the ring-plane normal, before and after. Saturn: every inner moon 26.73 deg off the ring plane before, now at its own inclination (Enceladus 0.01, Dione 0.02, Rhea 0.34, Titan 0.35, Tethys 1.12, Mimas 1.57), with Iapetus 30.60 -> 15.47 as the one odd moon out. Uranus 97.77 -> 0.06-0.34. Jupiter 3.13 -> 0.05-0.47. Triton 144.30 -> 157.30, its true retrograde figure. Luna unchanged at 23.97 deg from Earth's equatorial plane, which is inside the expected 18.3-28.6 band — an independent confirmation that the ecliptic flag does the right thing. **Applied to SATELLITES ONLY**, gated on the scene's `satellite` flag: barycentre members share a parent but are system-level and must not be rotated, and a planet's inclination is ecliptic-relative and is never touched. Both sites moved together — the moon's body position and its orbit ring — so the moon rides its own ring. **(c) proc gen is NOT done:** new moons and rings are still generated without inheriting their host's tilt. It needs the generator, not the renderer, and wants its own pass. **(c) DONE v2.1.366-beta, and half of it turned out to be already done by [[B10]].** **THE RENDERER NEVER NEEDED CHANGING — the hosts needed tilts.** `scene.ts` has always rotated a satellite's orbit by its parent's `axial_tilt_deg`, so "incline moons with their host" was a no-op only because no generated host HAD a tilt. Measured by reproducing `toParentEquator` over 80 generated moons: with host tilts zeroed, they sat a mean **3.21 deg** from the system plane (max 9.83) — flat, just their own inclination; with the tilts B10 now gives them, **mean 19.09, max 73.22**. The moons ride their hosts' equators because their hosts finally lean. That makes this entry a dependency of B10 rather than the renderer work it was written as. **WHAT ACTUALLY REMAINED IS THE SWITCH, and [[C5]] had already done its physics.** Beyond the Laplace radius the star's tide beats the host's bulge and the orbit follows the system plane — `r_L^5 = 2 J2 R^2 a_p^3 (M_p/M_star)` — so a generated moon past it now carries `orbit.frame = 'ecliptic'`. C5 concluded a continuous Laplace model buys nothing for the bundled maps because a catalogue quotes real inclinations, and that its value was entirely in proc gen; this is that place, and it is the whole of C5's payoff. **J2 IS NOT IN THE DATA MODEL AND NEVER WILL BE FOR AN INVENTED PLANET**, so it is estimated from the rotation the generator already rolled: `q = w^2 R^3 / GM` times a structure factor. Measured against the Solar System, J2/q is 0.10 Saturn, 0.12 Uranus, 0.13 Neptune, 0.17 Jupiter, 0.31 Earth, 0.43 Mars — centrally-condensed giants low, rocky bodies high — so two pack constants cover it. **The fifth root is what makes a crude J2 good enough:** being wrong by a factor of three moves the handover radius by 25%. **Measured over 80 moons, eight seeds, both generators: 19 (24%) are now ecliptic-framed and both paths fire.** The two populations OVERLAP in a/Rp — equatorial 1.5-22.2, ecliptic 5.9-81.8 — **and that overlap is the point**: the handover depends on the host's rotation, mass and distance from its star, not on a fixed multiple of its radius, so a threshold on a/Rp would have been a different and wrong model. The Solar System's own Laplace radii span 9.7 R_p (Earth) to 73.5 (Neptune) for the same reason. **The inclination DISTRIBUTION is deliberately unchanged** — the drawn 0-10 deg is plausible in either frame (Luna 5.1 to the ecliptic, Io 0.05 to Jupiter's equator). It was the reference plane that was wrong, not the angle. **RINGS need nothing, checked rather than assumed:** a generated ring has no `orbit` at all, sits at 1.5-2.5 host radii, and the renderer already lays it in the parent's equator — so it inherits by the same route the moons do. **The manual add-a-body route got the same switch**, by importing `laplaceRadiusAU` rather than reimplementing it. **`Orbit.frame` is typed for the first time** — it already shipped in both bundled maps and `scene.ts` already read it through `as any`, which is exactly why it drifted untyped. **`tests/output/solar-system-derived.json` is unchanged by this**; it does differ in the working tree from the concurrent tag session's `SystemProcessor` and fixture work, verified by removing these files, regenerating, and getting a byte-identical diff. D4d's kit test stays green. **STILL OPEN, and it is the one case the flag cannot express:** a ring around a very slowly rotating planet. r_L goes as `w^(2/5)`, so a Venus-like rotator's Laplace radius falls to roughly 0.7 of its own radius — every satellite is solar-dominated and even the RING should lie in the system plane. A ring has no orbit and therefore no `frame` to set, so the renderer lays it in the equator unconditionally. Rare, but real, and appended unnumbered below. Related: [[C5]], [[B10]], [[D4d]]. | fixed v2.1.316-beta (a,b); (c) fixed v2.1.366-beta |
| C4 | **ON-PLANET constructs sit at the planet's CENTRE instead of on its surface, and so do not turn with it.** Noticed on the starmaps generated for the rebuild. A surface construct should be glued to a fixed point on the surface and inherit the body's rotation period; instead it renders at the centre and stays still while the planet turns. **Partly verified — two different definitions of "surface construct" exist and they may not agree.** (1) DATA: `constructsOf` (`document/systemTopology.ts:66-70`) splits surface from orbiting on an authored field, `c.placement === 'surface'` — an explicit declaration. (2) GEOMETRY: the holo scene does not read that field at all; it DETECTS surface-lock by comparing the construct's distance against `physRadiusAu`, "the true physical radius in AU (for detecting surface-locked constructs)" (`holo/scene.ts:141-142`), then glues the construct to a fixed surface point and re-glues it every frame so it turns with the body (~1794, ~1930). So the scene infers what the data already states. **Most likely cause, NOT yet verified — check this first:** if `physRadiusAu` is absent or zero on the generated bodies the detection cannot fire, and a construct authored at distance 0 then renders exactly where it was put, which is the centre. That would also explain why it does not rotate: the re-glue at ~1930 only runs for constructs the lock engaged on. Relate to D2 — the rebuilt systems are data-poor by design, so a mechanism that depends on a derived field is exactly what breaks on them first. **The fix is probably not a scene fix.** An authored `placement: 'surface'` is a stronger statement than a geometric guess and should WIN: the scene should honour the declaration and place the construct on the surface, rather than re-deriving surface-ness from a radius that may not be there. Confirm which way round it actually is before building — **FIXED v2.1.315-beta. The hypothesis above was WRONG, and it IS a scene fix — the data is fine.** `physRadiusAu` was never absent: it has a 3000 km fallback (`scene.ts:1743`) and every host in question has a real radius. What actually excluded them was the guard `off > 1e-12` on the surface-lock branch. Read the shipped data and it is unambiguous — all seven surface constructs across both bundled maps (Ceres, Eros, Ganymede, Phoebe, Bellerophon, Hadley's Hope, The Derelict) declare `placement: "Surface"` and carry NO `orbit` at all, because that is the honest way to author a ground station. So `off` is 0, the lock branch skipped them, and the orbital branch below is gated on the SAME `off > 1e-12` — so nothing positioned them and they kept `tmpParent`, the parent's centre, and were never re-glued, hence motionless. The one case that most plainly means "on the ground" was the one case both branches refused. **Fix:** the scene now reads the declaration, using the same test as `constructsOf` (`String(placement).toLowerCase() === 'surface'`) so the document builder and the scene cannot disagree about which constructs are on the ground; a declared construct locks whatever its offset. Where there is no offset to say WHERE, the landing site is derived from the construct's id — deterministic across reloads, and spread by latitude as well as longitude so two stations on one moon do not stack. Verified numerically: all seven directions unit length, and the LV-426 pair 33.8 degrees apart. **NEW FINDING, and it is the other half of "does not turn":** a host with no `rotation_period_hours` has no `spinPeriodSec`, so its quaternion never changes and a correctly surface-locked construct still will not rotate. Altair IV has no rotation period; LV-426 has 50 h. On the rebuilt data-poor maps this half persists after this fix and is a DATA gap — see D2, which already lists `rotation_period_hours` as one of the fields those systems lack. **What I could NOT confirm:** a clean visual of a construct sitting on the limb. The construct is a fixed-screen-size sprite and the planet mesh wins the raycast across the whole disc, so the pick oracle cannot see it either before or after (confirmed by A/B-ing the fix off and sweeping both ways); at a large framing the icon is swallowed by the globe and at whole-system framing the offset is sub-pixel. Altair IV, the one host I could reach in the automated sweep, also has no spin, so nothing there could turn regardless. The causal chain is verified at both ends — data read from the shipped JSON, both branches read in the code — but the eyeball check on a spinning host (LV-426 or the Sol moons) is still worth doing. Original hypothesis text follows: check a generated map for what a surface construct carries, and whether `physRadiusAu` reaches the scene for those bodies. If the generator is simply not writing `placement`, this is a DATA item and belongs in bucket D. | fixed v2.1.315-beta |
| C6 | **The GM 2D orrery's body disc never spins — surface features hold a fixed orientation while the lighting tracks the star correctly.** Reported 2026-08-01 on Mercury: "it does not rotate at all, so the right face is not facing the star... the cratered side always faces the same direction". The player 3D view is fine, because a real sphere spinning is free there. **Low priority, said so explicitly ("no biggie"), but recorded because the cause is not what it looks like.** **Verified:** `catalogue/PlanetDisc.svelte` takes `body, ringed, ringDensity, size, suppress, lightAngle, showStamp` — **there is no rotation or spin-phase input of any kind.** What it does have is `lightAngle`, which the orrery passes as the true angle toward the star, so the TERMINATOR is already correct and tracks the body round its orbit; only the surface stays put. Feature positions come from a seeded PRNG per body id (`seeded(9001)` craters, `seeded(9002)`, and so on) under the stated intent that "every feature seeds its own positions so the look is stable" — stability was the goal, and staticness is the same thing seen from another angle. **THE TRAP, and the reason this is not a one-liner:** rotating the disc's features about its centre is not a spinning sphere, it is a spinning record. A real spin carries features ACROSS the face and compresses them into the limbs, so an honest fix needs feature positions treated as sphere longitude/latitude and projected per frame, or at minimum a horizontal traverse with limb foreshortening. A naive `rotate()` will look worse than the current stillness and should not be accepted as a fix. Inputs needed: `rotation_period_hours` and the current time — both already available, and both now trustworthy after [[B7]] and [[B9]]. **Note also this only shows when zoomed in:** `DISC_OVERLAY_MIN_R = 11` px gates the overlay, so nothing is promoted at whole-system framing (see [[A10]]). **DITCHED 2026-08-03.** The lighting is correct, which is the part carrying the physics — a reader sees the body lit from its star, and the terminator tracks the orbit. Only the seeded surface features are static, and the honest fix is a spherical projection (features carried across the face with limb foreshortening) rather than the flat rotation that would be reached for first, which looks worse than stillness. Not worth that work at this priority. Reopen only if it draws real complaints. Related: [[A10]], [[B7]]. | wont-fix — lighting is right, and the cheap fix looks worse |
| C2 | `planetAppearance.ts` resolves deck liquids via `liquidDef(species)` with NO rulepack argument, so a campaign's own custom liquids never reach the 3D deck renderer. Worked around by putting `taumoeba-bloom` / `iron-oxide-dust` in the global list. If custom liquids are a real authoring surface, this needs fixing. | triaged |

## D — Content / data
