| B19 | **Mars is tagged "Stability · Fated: flung out". It is not going anywhere.** Sanity check 2026-08-01. Mars's orbit is stable on any timescale a game cares about; the inner Solar System shows only mild chaos over billions of years. A prediction of ejection points at the test, not at Mars. **Where it comes from:** `physics/stability.ts:146` — `if (delta < 5.5 && !isResonanceProtected(inner) && !isResonanceProtected(outer)) out.fate = 'eject'` — a MUTUAL HILL SEPARATION test, with other eject paths at `:126` (mass-ratio dependent) and `:362`. The 5.5 threshold is itself defensible: the literature puts long-term instability below roughly ten mutual Hill radii for comparable masses, so 5.5 is conservative rather than aggressive, and the constant is probably not the fault. **HYPOTHESIS, NOT VERIFIED — CHECK IT FIRST:** Mars against Earth is about 26 mutual Hill radii and could not trip this. But Mars's outward NEIGHBOUR IN ORBIT ORDER IS THE ASTEROID BELT, and [[B11]] has just established that belts return early from the third derivation pass. If a belt node carries a mass and is included in the neighbour pairing, then the mutual Hill radius is being computed between a planet and a diffuse belt, which is not a two-body problem and will produce nonsense. Confirm what Mars is actually being paired with before touching any constant — print the pair and the delta. **If that is it, the fix is about which nodes are ELIGIBLE for a pairwise stability test, not about the threshold**, and it will affect every system with a belt between planets rather than Mars alone. Do not "fix" this by raising 5.5 until the pairing is proven sound. Related: [[B11]]. **FIXED v2.1.338-beta. THE HYPOTHESIS IS REFUTED AND THE THRESHOLD IS INNOCENT — both checked before anything changed.** **Belts were already excluded.** `annotateGravitationalStability` filters `roleHint` 'belt' and 'ring' out of `orbitalNodes` before any pairing, with a comment naming this exact hazard ("their massKg is a debris-density proxy, not a point mass"), and `stability.spec.ts` already had a test for it. No planet is being paired against a diffuse belt. **The 5.5 threshold never fires here.** Printed the pair and the delta first, as the entry demanded: Mars's inward neighbour in the starmap Sol is **433 EROS**, a 16 km near-Earth asteroid carried as `roleHint: 'planet'` at a = 1.458, and the pair sits at **DELTA = 9.28** — well outside 5.5. The constant is untouched. **The real cause is the ORBIT-OVERLAP branch (`stability.ts:95-126`), and it is one line below where anyone was looking.** Eros's eccentric orbit genuinely does cross Mars's, so the crossing test fires correctly; with a mass ratio of ~1e-8 it correctly returns `eject` rather than `collision`. Then `mergeAssessment` copied that fate onto **BOTH** members of the pair. **The code's own comments already stated the intent** — "a lightweight crosser is scattered out", "packed systems shed their lighter member" — so the model always knew which body it meant and simply never recorded it. **Fix: `StabilityAssessment` gains `fateNodeId`**, set to the lighter body on both eject paths (crossing and Hill spacing) and left unset for a `collision`, which between comparable masses genuinely IS mutual. Severity and reasons still merge onto both, because both bodies really are in a risky pair — only the FATE is directional. **Measured over every bundled system: 29 bodies tagged `fate/eject` -> 22, and all seven that dropped it were the heavier half of their pair.** Mars keeps its metastable severity and loses the verdict; Eros keeps it, which is right. GJ 876 is the clean demonstration — b at 4.32e27 kg drops it while c at 1.36e27 and e at 8.72e25 keep it. **`tests/output/solar-system-derived.json` is UNCHANGED, correctly:** the baseline is built from `static/examples/Sol_2030-System.json`, which contains no Eros — the reported body lives in the starmap Sol. The sweep above is the measurement. Pinned by three tests, one of which asserts the delta is outside 5.5 so nobody re-blames the threshold. **Two things noticed and NOT fixed:** several bodies carry a reason reading "...so it stays stable" beside "Predicted outcome: flung out", because the fate came from a different driver than the reason that is printed next to it ([[B24]]); and for two nearly equal masses on the Hill path "lighter" is close to arbitrary, though naming one is still more honest than naming both. | fixed v2.1.338-beta |
| B18 | **Uranus scores 50% habitability — above Mars at 8% and Enceladus at 35%.** Sanity check 2026-08-01. Uranus is an ice giant with no solid surface, crushing pressures at depth, violent winds and extreme cold at the cloud decks. Even on the most generous atmospheric-envelope reading it should not outrank a rocky world with a real surface or a moon with a subsurface ocean. The RANKING is the evidence here, more than the absolute number: whatever the score means, Mars and Enceladus should sit above Uranus on it, and they do not. **Suspect a weighting artefact rather than a wrong input** — something over-rewarding atmospheric mass, volatile presence or air mix, which a gas giant trivially maximises while a thin-aired rocky world scores near zero on all three. **Where to start:** `SystemProcessor.ts:1170` clamps `habitabilityScore` to 0-130, with a subsurface-niche floor at `:1179-1180` (`SUBSURFACE_NICHE_SCORE`) and an alien-habitable gate at `:1188` requiring score > 40 and `factors.solvent > 0`. The factor weights feeding that score are the thing to audit — I have not read them, so treat the artefact diagnosis as a starting hypothesis rather than a finding. **Check whether a body with NO SOLID SURFACE should be scoring on this axis at all**: the subsurface-niche floor shows the model already accepts that habitability can mean something other than "stand on it", so the question is whether a giant's envelope is a niche the model means to score, or an accident of factors designed for surfaces. Answer that before adjusting weights, or the weights will be tuned to hide a category error. Sanity anchors to hold the result against once changed: Earth top, Mars low but non-zero, Enceladus and Europa meaningful via the subsurface niche, Venus low, the giants below all of them. **FIXED v2.1.339-beta. IT IS A CATEGORY ERROR, NOT A WEIGHTING ARTEFACT — the category question is answered first, and answering it made the weights irrelevant.** **The axis is a SURFACE model and says so throughout:** the running total is literally named `surfaceScore`, every factor is a surface condition, and `isEarthLike` / `isHumanHabitable` all test what it is like to stand there. **The proof that the model MEANS surface is the subsurface niche itself** — non-surface habitability is handled by ADDING an explicitly named niche with its own floor and its own tier, precisely because these factors cannot express it. So the model had already answered the question: a niche that is not a surface gets named and scored separately, rather than being left to surface factors. A giant's envelope is therefore outside what this axis measures. **Which factor does a giant trivially maximise? All three that survive, and the arithmetic is exact.** Uranus scores **temp 0, solvent 0** — the two worth 50 of the 100 points, and the two that actually decide the question — then **pressure 18 + radiation 17 + gravity 15 = 50**. Neptune is identical, Saturn 49.9, Jupiter 30 (it loses gravity at 2.64 g). **And all three are evaluated at a level that is not a surface:** a giant's `atmosphere.pressure_bar` is pinned to the 1-bar cloud-top reference (a deliberate simplification, ARCHITECTURE.md), and its "surface" radiation and "surface" gravity are computed at that same notional radius. The three factors are not over-weighted — they are being asked about a place that does not exist. Tuning them would have buried that. **Gated on `makeupFractions(planet).gas <= 0.5`, the same test the geology model already uses**, so the two agree on what a surface is. A caught body scores 0 and its Bio tab reads "No solid surface — surface habitability does not apply" instead of a blank panel or a silent 50. **Every anchor the entry set now holds:** Earth 100 (earth-like) > Titan 47 (alien — it has a real surface and real liquid methane) > Europa 35 = Enceladus 35 (subsurface niche) > Mars 8 > Venus 7 > **all four giants 0**. **Swept all 366 bundled planets and moons: 59 caught, every one a giant / neptune / puffy class, and ZERO of them have a surface liquid** — which is the check that would have exposed a false positive. `planet/hycean` is NOT caught, so an ocean under a thick H2 envelope keeps its score. 52 of the 59 were previously scoring over 10 from the surfaceless factors alone. **Derived diff is four bodies and only the giants: 30/50/50/50 -> 0. Their TIER does not change** — it was already `habitability/none`, because `isAlienHabitable` requires `factors.solvent > 0` and they never had any. **Only the NUMBER ever lied**, which is why this survived so long: the tag was right the whole time. **Deliberately NOT done:** whether a giant's ENVELOPE deserves an aerial niche of its own, the way the subsurface ocean has one. That is a design decision rather than a bug fix, and inventing one unasked would repeat the same mistake in the other direction. Also raised: [[B25]]. | fixed v2.1.339-beta |
| B17 | **Io's radiation is far too low — nothing models radiation from a NON-STELLAR source.** Sanity check 2026-08-01. Io shows `moderate (19.2-2... mSv/y)` and tags `Irradiation: low`. In reality Io sits deep inside Jupiter's inner magnetosphere and its energetic particle belt: unshielded surface dose is of order 36 Sv per DAY, around 13,000 Sv/year, making it one of the most violently irradiated surfaces in the Solar System. The engine is out by something like six orders of magnitude, and it is out because the source does not exist in the model rather than because a number is mistuned. **The engine computes radiation from STARS only** — `calculateSurfaceRadiation` and `calculateTotalStellarRadiationRange` are stellar by construction (see [[B8]], which fixed a missing flare term in that same stellar path). **The interesting part, and the reason this is not simply "a missing feature":** the engine ALREADY KNOWS a body can sit inside a giant's magnetosphere. `SystemProcessor.ts:765-768` computes `insideHostMagnetosphere` from the host's mass, gas fraction and field strength, and feeds it to `deriveMagnetism` — but it is used only to influence the MOON'S OWN magnetism, never as a radiation source. So the concept is present and pointed the wrong way: being inside a magnetosphere is currently treated purely as context for a dynamo, when for a close-in moon of a strong-field giant it is the dominant HAZARD. That existing flag is the natural hook. **Scope honestly before building:** a real magnetospheric dose model needs the host's field strength, the moon's orbital distance in host radii, and some belt profile — and it should degrade gracefully to zero for the many hosts with no meaningful field. It will change Io, Europa, Ganymede and Callisto (in that steep order) and essentially nothing else in the bundled maps, which makes it easy to validate and hard to get accidentally wrong elsewhere. Anchors exist for all four Galileans, so this is a model that can be CALIBRATED rather than guessed — the same standard [[B2]] was held to. **THE INPUTS ALREADY EXIST AND ARE ALREADY ACCURATE — checked in `tests/output/solar-system-derived.json`, so this is far cheaper than "a new physics model" sounds.** A magnetosphere is powered by FIELD x SPIN, and the engine already derives both for every giant: Jupiter `strengthGauss` **4.32** against a measured ~4.3 and rotation 9.925 h; Earth 0.5014 against ~0.5; Saturn 0.2396 (~0.21) at 10.656 h; Uranus 0.2088 (~0.23) at **-17.24 h, retrograde with the correct sign**; Neptune 0.2235 (~0.14) at 16.11 h. Nothing new needs authoring or placeholding — `deriveMagnetism` is already producing the numbers and the radiation path simply never asks for them. **The clean diagnostic, worth using as the before/after test:** Io's `surfaceRadiation` is 20.9397 and Europa's is 20.9276 — near-identical, because solar distance is the only term in the model, while in reality Io takes roughly SEVEN TIMES Europa's dose. Two moons agreeing to four significant figures is the fingerprint of the missing source, and separating them is the headline result. Io's own `strengthGauss` is 0, correctly — it has no dynamo, so all of its dose is external. **Shape of the model:** dose as a function of (host field, host spin) x a steep falloff in orbital distance measured in HOST RADII — the Galilean anchors imply something like an inverse sixth-to-eighth power between 5.9 and 26 R_J. Saturn is the second system and the real test of the field term, since its structure is the same and its field is roughly eighteen times weaker, so Enceladus and Titan must come out far lower than the Galileans rather than merely proportionally lower. Must degrade to zero for hosts with no meaningful field, which is most of them. **DO NOT BUILD THIS AS "A PLANET EMITS RADIATION" — that was proposed 2026-08-01 and it is the wrong mechanism, for a reason that is decisive rather than stylistic.** Three things are easily conflated here and only one is missing. **(a) Self-luminous bodies are ALREADY DONE.** `physics/substellar.ts` models them from mass, system age and radius, exposes `luminositySolar` commented "for irradiating moons", and `isLuminousSource` (`:51-52`) is `roleHint === 'star' || isSelfLuminous` — so a substellar object already joins the same `radiationOutput / d^2` sum as the stars in `calculateTotalStellarRadiation`. The flag is set by the processor's substellar pass (`SystemProcessor.ts:336/347`) and the model's table starts at **13 M_jup**, the deuterium-burning limit. Brown dwarfs are covered; the sum was always a predicate over emitters rather than a hardcoded star check. **(b) Jupiter is correctly EXCLUDED and must stay so.** At ~1 M_jup it is an order of magnitude under that floor, and its real excess output (about 1.67x absorbed sunlight, from Kelvin-Helmholtz contraction) is INFRARED and non-ionising. It is a heat source, not a dose source. **(c) THE ARITHMETIC THAT SETTLES IT.** An emitter-plus-distance model is inverse-square, `r^-2`. The Galilean anchors demand a falloff between **`r^-4`** (Io to Europa: 1.59x the distance, 6.7x less dose) and **`r^-8.6`** (Io to Callisto: 4.4x the distance, 360,000x less dose). No inverse-square source can fit those: calibrate at Io and Callisto is wrong by orders of magnitude, calibrate at Callisto and Io is. That is because the dose is not radiation FROM Jupiter but charged particles TRAPPED BY its field and accelerated by its rotation — a peaked belt profile in planetary radii, which is a different law and needs its own TERM rather than a new entry in the luminous-source sum. **CORRECTED AND SHARPENED 2026-08-01 — "surely we can work out its likely radiation output, magnetic field driven, all procedural and reusable" is RIGHT, and the objection above applies only to the DISTANCE LAW, not to the idea of a derived planetary source.** The intended chain — rotation and composition give a field, the field drives a source strength, that source is summed at the receiving body — is exactly correct and is what this item should build. **And the sum it needs ALREADY EXISTS.** `calculateStellarRadiationComponents` (`radiation.ts:55-82`) splits every source into a PHOTON and a PARTICLE channel, and the particle channel is already attenuated by the receiver's magnetosphere and atmosphere: "Goes in the particle channel so a magnetosphere + atmosphere shield against it (an unshielded close world bears the brunt)." Trapped belt radiation is a PURE particle-channel source with no photon component, so it drops straight into existing, already-shielded machinery. `insideHostMagnetosphere` (`SystemProcessor.ts:765-768`) is already computed and is the natural gate. **So the whole delta is ONE TERM: a belt strength derived from host field x spin, delivered into the particle channel with a falloff in HOST RADII rather than `1/d^2`.** Nothing about the architecture needs changing and no body needs a special case. **CALIBRATION HAZARD, and it is the same shape as the Mars albedo trap in [[B5]]:** Ganymede is the only moon in the Solar System with its own dynamo (~0.0072 G measured) and that shield is part of why its dose sits where it does — but the engine currently gives it `strengthGauss: 0`. Calibrate the belt profile against Ganymede's measured dose without modelling its shield and the shielding gets baked into the belt law, giving a fit that is right for the wrong reason. **Calibrate on Io and Callisto, neither of which has a field, then treat Ganymede's shield as a PREDICTION to check.** Related: [[B8]], [[B12]], [[B5]]. **FIXED v2.1.340-beta.** **Root cause in a sentence: the engine only ever summed LUMINOUS sources, so the dominant hazard for a close-in moon of a strong-field giant had no representation at all** — not a mistuned number but an absent mechanism, and the fingerprint was Io 20.9397 against Europa 20.9276, two moons agreeing to four significant figures because solar distance was the only term either had. **THE SUBSTELLAR PATH WAS VERIFIED END TO END FIRST, as the brief demanded, rather than trusted from the predicate.** Measured on a synthetic host: at 1 M_jup the moon sees 2.01 mSv/yr; at 13 M_jup `isSelfLuminous` flips and it sees **104.4**, then 351 at 20 M_jup and 2626 at 40. The path works, Jupiter at ~1 M_jup is correctly excluded, and the new term sits beside it without touching it. **THE LAW, and its shape is forced by the anchors rather than chosen:** `dose(r) = D0 · (B/B_ref)² · (Ω/Ω_ref) · exp(−r/λ)` with `λ = λ_ref · (B/B_ref)^(1/3)`, r in HOST RADII, delivered into the particle channel. An emitter at a distance is inverse-square; the Galileans demand r^-4 from Io to Europa and r^-8.6 from Io to Callisto, and no power law fits both — but a belt is not a point source, it is a population confined by a field, and **one exponential fits the entire family**. **The exponents are REASONED, NOT FITTED** — there is only one calibrated system, so fitting them would be overfitting: B² is the magnetic energy density available to trap, Ω is the corotation drive that energises the particles, and a dipole's magnetopause standoff goes as B^(1/3), so a weaker host holds a TIGHTER belt as well as a fainter one. Constants live in `generation_parameters` as rule-pack data. No body is special-cased. **CALIBRATED ON IO AND CALLISTO ONLY** — the two Galileans with no field of their own, so no self-shielding is baked into the law. Two anchors, two free parameters, using the ENGINE's radii rather than textbook ones: **λ = 1.6324 R_J, D0 = 1451.1 Sv/day**. | body | r (R_J) | predicted | measured | ratio | | |---|---|---|---|---|---| | Io | 6.03 | 3.600e+1 | 3.600e+1 | 1.00 | fitted | | Europa | 9.59 | 4.086e+0 | 5.400e+0 | **0.76** | PREDICTED | | Ganymede | 15.30 | 1.234e-1 | 8.000e-2 | **1.54** | PREDICTED, held out | | Callisto | 26.92 | 1.000e-4 | 1.000e-4 | 1.00 | fitted | **GANYMEDE, the deliberate hold-out, came in 1.54x HIGH — and high is the right direction**, because it is the only moon in the Solar System with its own dynamo (~0.0072 G) and the law does not model that shield. Avoiding it as an anchor is what kept its shielding out of the belt profile; had it been fitted, the law would have been right for the wrong reason. **THE DERIVED DIFF: 18 bodies, all moons and rings of the four giants, and only radiation fields** plus one habitability knock-on (radiation is a habitability factor — only Ariel moves, 2 -> 0). No class and no temperature moved anywhere. `Io 20.940 -> 1.27501e+7 mSv/yr` (34.9 Sv/day against a 36 anchor) and `Europa 20.928 -> 1.43732e+6` (3.94 against 5.4). **Io/Europa is now 8.9x where it was 1.0006x** — that separation is the headline result. Jupiter's Rings top the list at 352 Sv/day, which is correct rather than alarming: the main ring sits near 1.8 R_J, inside Io and close to the belt peak. **SATURN, the field term's real test: Enceladus 0.0034 Sv/day and Titan effectively nothing, against Io's 34.9.** Enceladus lands **~10,000x below Io where the field ratio alone is only 18x**, because the B² intensity and the tighter B^(1/3) scale length compound. Far lower rather than merely proportionally lower, as required. **Degrades to zero where it should:** Luna sits 60 Earth radii out and gets nothing; Earth, Triton, Titania and Oberon are unchanged to five significant figures. It returns 0 for an unknown host SPIN too — an absent rotation is not a claim of a stationary host, and inventing a hazard from a missing input is worse than omitting one ([[B9]]). **[[B8]]'s invariant survives**: 0 of 34 bodies outside their own min/max, because the belt goes through the same three-way component call. **NOT DONE, said plainly.** (a) **Ganymede's authored 0.007 G is overwritten by `deriveMagnetism`'s 0, yet `radiationShieldingMag` reads 0.0768** — pass 2 shielded it with the authored value before pass 3 zeroed it. That is **[[B13]] visible in a single body**, and it means the engine's Ganymede figure (0.1086 Sv/day) is partly shielded by a field the same output reports as absent; the pure law gives 0.1234. Left for B13. **CLOSED by [[B13]] at v2.1.356-beta: Ganymede now reads 0.1234 Sv/day, the pure-law figure, and the fix also put IO back on its own anchor at 36.00 Sv/day where it had been reading 34.9 — the belt was evaluating Jupiter's AUTHORED 4.28 G while this calibration used the DERIVED 4.32. Callisto went the other way, to 1.06e-4 against its 1e-4 anchor, so the two-point fit is now 6% out at the far end and should be re-derived at 4.32 if that is worth closing.** (b) Whether a RING should carry a "surface" dose at all — it already did before this change, so it is not new, but it is odd. (c) [[B20]]'s radiation decomposition, which depends on this landing first. | fixed v2.1.340-beta |
| B20 | **Radiation should be decomposed BY SOURCE AND BY CAUSE, with a range — exactly as temperature already is.** Requested 2026-08-01: "radiation data blocks that detail the summing and sources. Perhaps even ranges if that is more accurate — like temp (tidally locked stars may have low radiation on non-star facing side)". **This is a MIRROR of an existing pattern, not a new subsystem, and it should be built as one.** `types.ts:36-38` already defines `TempSource = 'latitude' | 'seasonal' | 'diurnal' | 'locked-day' | 'locked-night' | 'tidal-hotspot'`, `TempComponent { source, label, lowK, highK, note? }` and `SurfaceTempProfile { meanK, totalMinK, totalMaxK, components[] }`, with the header comment stating the case for it: each component is the swing that source ALONE would produce around the mean, and the totals are the combined worst-case extremes. `BodyTemperatureTab.svelte:222` renders it with `{#each p.components as c}`. A `SurfaceRadiationProfile` should take the same shape, share the same cause vocabulary where it overlaps (`locked-day`/`locked-night`/`diurnal` all apply), and REUSE that renderer generalised rather than copied. **Two halves, and they are very different sizes.** **(1) Mostly restructuring:** the per-SOURCE itemisation. `calculateStellarRadiationComponents` (`radiation.ts:55-82`) already loops every luminous source and already splits photon/particle per source — it just ACCUMULATES into two running totals. Keeping the per-source rows instead of collapsing them gives "Alpha Centauri A contributes X, B contributes Y" for free, which is genuinely useful in a binary and currently impossible to see. The orbital-distance RANGE also already exists: the `where: 'current' | 'near' | 'far'` parameter computes periapsis and apoapsis flux, which is what [[B8]] rebuilt the endpoints from. **(2) Genuinely new physics:** the day/night and locked-face variation. **AND THE PHYSICS CAUTION THAT MAKES THE WHOLE BREAKDOWN WORTH HAVING:** "low on the far side" is TRUE for the PHOTON channel — the body's own bulk geometrically shadows the night side from starlight — and MUCH LESS TRUE for the PARTICLE channel, because charged particles spiral along magnetic field lines and arrive from all directions. So a locked world has a genuinely safe night side under a quiet star, and effectively none inside a giant's belt ([[B17]]). A single min/max cannot express that; a by-cause decomposition can, and it is the argument for doing this properly rather than just widening a range. **Do this AFTER [[B17]]**, so the belt term exists as a source to decompose rather than needing retrofitting into the block afterwards. **THE DEEPER POINT, AND IT CHANGES THE BUILD — "heat is just radiation much of the time".** These two profiles are not merely similar in SHAPE, they share INPUTS: the same emitters, the same `1/d^2`, the same periapsis/apoapsis range, the same locked-face and day/night geometry. So the correct structure is ONE flux-and-geometry layer feeding TWO derived profiles, not two parallel systems that happen to resemble each other. Building them separately is how they drift, and that exact failure has now happened twice in this engine: [[B8]] was literally two sums of the same quantity disagreeing, and [[B1]] was `albedo.ts` running a rival cloud model against the documented single evaluation. **THE ONE PLACE THEY MUST STAY DISTINCT — do not let the shared layer collapse them.** Temperature is driven by ABSORBED BOLOMETRIC flux, weighted by albedo and then REDISTRIBUTED by atmosphere and rotation. Dose is driven by the IONISING slice (UV/X-ray plus charged particles), a small spectrally distinct fraction, and it is ATTENUATED by atmosphere and magnetosphere rather than absorbed and spread around. The clean illustration: **Venus's night side is as hot as its day side, because a thick atmosphere transports the heat — while its night-side UV dose is zero.** Same body, same star, same geometry, profiles that diverge completely. Share the geometry, never the quantity. Related: [[B8]], [[B1]], [[B17]], [[A5]]. | captured |
| B21 | **Draw RADIATION ZONES around dangerously emitting planets, the way stellar zones are already drawn, and make routes passing through them say so.** Requested 2026-08-01, immediately after [[B17]] shipped. Three surfaces, and all three already have the machinery — this is an extension, not a new subsystem. **(1) THE PHYSICS IS FREE.** `physics/zones.ts` already computes stellar zones — `calculateKillZone(star)` (`:31`), `calculateGoldilocksZone` (`:103`), frost/soot/silicate/CO2/CO ice lines, and `calculateAllStellarZones` (`:285`). B17's belt law is `dose(r) = D0 x (B/B_ref)^2 x (Omega/Omega_ref) x exp(-r/lambda)` with `lambda = lambda_ref x (B/B_ref)^(1/3)`, calibrated to `lambda = 1.6324 R_J`, `D0 = 1451.1 Sv/day`. That INVERTS ANALYTICALLY: solve for a dose threshold and the zone radius is `r = lambda x ln(...)`. So a planetary hazard radius is one line of algebra against a law that already exists — no new model, no new fitting, and it automatically degrades to nothing for the many hosts with no meaningful field, exactly as the dose does. **Pick the THRESHOLD as rule-pack DATA, not a code constant**, and consider more than one band the way the stellar zones are banded rather than a single on/off radius — "lethal in hours" and "lethal in weeks" are different tactical facts for a GM. **(2) DRAWING IT.** The stellar zones already render; a planetary zone is the same idea centred on a planet rather than a star, so the question is reuse rather than invention. Watch the scale problem: a belt is a few host radii across, which at whole-system framing is sub-pixel, so it will need the same screen-space treatment the true-scale work uses ([[A9]], [[A19]]) or it will be invisible exactly when a GM is looking at the system as a whole. **(3) ROUTES.** `transit/telemetry.ts` already carries `HazardEvent[]` (`:14`) and already pushes hazards for rings and local dangers (`:85-142`), so a leg crossing a belt raises one through existing plumbing. **Do better than a binary crossing flag if it is cheap:** dose is an integral over TIME SPENT INSIDE, so a fast flyby and a slow spiral through the same zone are very different, and the telemetry already knows the leg's timing. An accumulated dose for the transit is far more useful at the table than "route passes through radiation". **Sequencing:** wants [[B20]] at least designed first, since the decomposition defines how a belt contribution is named and surfaced, and a route hazard should speak the same language as the body's own radiation block. **THE HOST ITSELF TAKES NO BELT DOSE, AND THAT IS THE CASE THIS ITEM HAS TO ANSWER.** Asked directly 2026-08-01 — "are we forgetting to apply it to itself?" — and the answer is yes, structurally: `beltParticleFlux` (`radiation.ts:71`) resolves its host as `body.parentId` (`:84`), so a PLANET'S host is its STAR and a giant is never inside its own belt. Jupiter consequently reads 11.5 mSv/yr, lower than Mars. For the quoted 1-bar "surface" that is roughly the right answer anyway, since the overlying atmosphere would absorb the particles — but it is right by EXCLUSION rather than by shielding, and the distinction matters here. **A ship skimming Jupiter's cloud tops is inside the belt and currently reads as safe, and Jupiter carries a `frontier/gas-skimming` tag**, so this is a real route-planning case rather than a theoretical one. Whatever zone model this item builds must treat the host's own upper atmosphere as INSIDE the hazard, not outside it — the zone starts at the cloud tops, not at the host's orbit. Related: [[B17]], [[B20]], [[A9]]. | captured, feature |
| B22 | **A GIANT IS NEVER INSIDE ITS OWN BELT: Jupiter reads 11.5 mSv/yr, less than Mars, at the centre of the most intense radiation environment in the Solar System.** Reported 2026-08-01 as "radiation is super high on Io but lowish on Jupiter — are we forgetting to apply it to itself?" Yes, and structurally rather than by a tuning error. **VERIFIED:** `beltParticleFlux` (`radiation.ts:71`) resolves its host as `body.parentId` (`:84`), so a PLANET'S host is its STAR. Jupiter's belt term is therefore evaluated against Sol — 1610 solar radii away, where the exponential is zero — and Jupiter's own 4.32 G field never irradiates anything but its moons. **The shipped law already gives the right answer; it is simply never asked.** Run it at Jupiter's own cloud tops: r = 1 R_J against `lambda = 1.6324 R_J` gives `exp(-0.613) x 1451.1 = about 786 Sv/day`, roughly twenty times Io's 36 Sv/day — which is correct, because Io orbits at 5.9 R_J, well outside the belt peak. Nothing needs calibrating; the host case needs including. **THE SECOND, DEEPER FAULT, and it should be settled before the first is patched:** quoting a SURFACE radiation for a gas giant is a category error, and this engine has already ruled on exactly that reasoning — [[B18]] shipped at v2.1.339 on the grounds that "a gas giant has no surface, so it does not score on a surface axis". The same argument applies here. The figure a GM needs for Jupiter is not a surface dose but the environment where things actually GO: the cloud tops and orbital space. So the fix is not merely "evaluate the belt at r = 1 host radius" — it is deciding what radiation figure a surfaceless body should report at all, and at what reference altitude, then making the belt available there. **Ties directly to [[B21]]:** a ship skimming Jupiter's cloud tops is deep inside the belt and currently reads as safe, and Jupiter carries a `frontier/gas-skimming` tag, so this is a live route-planning case rather than a curiosity. **Do this with or before [[B20]]** — a decomposition that cannot express "this body has no surface, here is the dose at the 1-bar level and here is the dose in orbit" will need reworking as soon as this lands. **DECIDED 2026-08-01: report BOTH, LABELLED.** A surfaceless body quotes two named figures — the 1-bar reference level AND the cloud-top / orbital environment — because the ambiguity is real and hiding it behind one number is what produced this report. **Build it as the general two-figure shape that [[B20]] will extend, NOT as a giant-specific special case**, or B20 rewrites it immediately. **STOP: THE OBVIOUS IMPLEMENTATION MAKES EARTH LETHAL. Read this before writing any code.** If the fix is "let a body evaluate the belt against its OWN field at r = 1 host radius", run the shipped law on Earth's own numbers: `B/B_ref = 0.5014/4.32 = 0.116` so `(B/B_ref)^2 = 0.0135`; spin ratio `9.925/23.934 = 0.415`; `lambda = 1.6324 x 0.116^(1/3) = 0.797 R_E`; `exp(-1/0.797) = 0.285`. With `D0 = 1451.1 Sv/day` that is **2.31 Sv/day at Earth's surface**, and even after atmospheric attenuation (`exp(-7.09) = 8.3e-4`) it is about 700 mSv/yr against a real background of 2.4 mSv/yr — nearly 300x too high, on the single best-calibrated body in the engine. **The physics that the law is missing is an INNER EDGE.** Real radiation belts do not reach the surface: the atmosphere absorbs trapped particles into the loss cone, which is why Earth's inner belt begins around 1.2 R_E rather than at the ground. A bare `exp(-r/lambda)` has no lower boundary and will happily report the belt peak at the centre of the planet. So B22 is NOT "evaluate at r = 1" — it needs a belt inner boundary, below which the trapped population is absorbed rather than merely attenuated. That boundary is also what makes the cloud-top figure meaningful rather than arbitrary. **Earth at ~2.4 mSv/yr and Mars at ~213 mSv/yr are the regression tests; neither may move.** Related: [[B17]], [[B18]], [[B20]], [[B21]], [[A33]]. **FIXED v2.1.341-beta.** **Root cause in a sentence: `beltParticleFlux` resolves its host as `body.parentId`, so a PLANET's host is its STAR** — Jupiter's belt was evaluated against Sol at 1610 solar radii where the exponential is zero, and its own 4.32 G field irradiated nothing but its moons. The law was already right; it was simply never asked. **THE INNER EDGE IS THE PART THAT MATTERS, and the warning in this entry was correct — the obvious implementation does make Earth lethal.** A bare `exp(-r/λ)` has no lower boundary, so asked about a body's own field it reports the belt PEAK at the centre of the planet: **2.31 Sv/day at Earth's ground, ~300x the real background**, on the best-calibrated body in the engine. **What it derives from, and it is not a picked constant:** real belts stop well above the surface because the ATMOSPHERE absorbs trapped particles into the loss cone — a particle whose mirror point lies in dense air is gone within one bounce rather than merely attenuated. So the boundary is a property of the atmosphere, and the engine already derives the only quantity it needs: **`atmosphere.scaleHeightKm` (H = RT/gM)**. The edge sits a fixed number of SCALE HEIGHTS above the reference level, which makes it scale with the AIR rather than with the planet — a puffy hot atmosphere holds its belt further out, a thin one lets it closer, and an **airless body has no absorber at all so its belt reaches the ground** (which is why Ganymede's poles are scoured by precipitating particles). The scale-height count is rule-pack DATA, calibrated on the one inner edge that is well measured — **Earth's, near 1.2 R_E, giving 150 H**. Jupiter is then a CHECK rather than a fit: 1.048 R_J, 764 Sv/day, about 21x Io, which is the region this entry independently predicted. **TWO NAMED FIGURES, built general rather than giant-specific so [[B20]] can extend it.** `surfaceRadiation` is the ground, or for a surfaceless body the 1-bar reference level; `orbitalRadiation` is the environment above the atmosphere, where a magnetised body sits inside its own belt. **Every body gets both** — it is just as true of Earth, whose orbital space contains the Van Allen belts and whose surface does not. Labelled in the stat block: "Radiation (surface)" against "Radiation (at 1 bar)", with the orbital row printed only where it genuinely differs. | body | 1-bar / surface | in orbit | inner edge | |---|---|---|---| | Jupiter | 11.4655 mSv/y | **747.2 Sv/day** | 1.0499 R_J | | Saturn | 5.0929 mSv/y | 0.4859 Sv/day | 1.1219 R_S | | Uranus | 2.6641 mSv/y | 0.3500 Sv/day | 1.1740 R_U | | Neptune | 2.2773 mSv/y | 0.1083 Sv/day | 1.1240 R_N | | Earth | 2.2990 mSv/y | 1.789 Sv/day | 1.1982 R_E | **Jupiter/Saturn at the cloud tops is 1538x where the field ratio alone is 18x** — far lower rather than proportionally lower, because the B² intensity and the tighter B^(1/3) scale length compound, exactly as [[B17]] predicted they would. **EVERY REGRESSION ANCHOR IS UNMOVED TO THE LAST DIGIT: Earth 2.29899, Mars 213.692, Io 1.27501e+7 (34.93 Sv/day), Europa 1.43732e+6, Ganymede 39657.2, Callisto 54.9044.** And **the derived diff is PURE ADDITION — 68 insertions, 0 deletions**, only `orbitalRadiation` and `beltInnerEdgeRadii` on all 34 bodies. Not one existing value moved, because the inner edge absorbs the self-belt at r = 1 for every body that has an atmosphere. That is the strongest form the "must not move" test could have taken. **One bug caught by MEASUREMENT rather than review, worth recording because it would have looked plausible:** `components.particle` already carries the belt terms from B17, so the first orbital figure double-counted the host belt and every Galilean read **exactly 2.000x** its surface dose. An exact factor of two is the giveaway. The stellar particle component is now separated out before deflection, and the belt terms are deliberately NOT magnetospherically deflected in the orbital figure — a body's own field does not shield it from the belt that field is holding in place. **NOT DONE, said plainly.** (a) [[B20]]'s full by-source decomposition, which this was shaped to accept rather than pre-empt. (b) **Saturn's real inner edge is set by RING absorption, not by its atmosphere** — its rings sweep up trapped particles, which is why its belts genuinely start near 2.3 R_S rather than the 1.12 R_S this model gives. The consequence is that Saturn's cloud-top figure is an over-estimate; it is still ~1500x below Jupiter's, so no conclusion here rests on it, but a ring-absorption term is the obvious next refinement and it ties to [[B26]]. (c) The old B22 (a ring carrying a surface dose) collided with this entry's number and is renumbered [[B26]]. | fixed v2.1.341-beta |
| B23 | **CHECKED, NO CHANGE RECOMMENDED: should stellar radiation be derived from stellar spin and field now that planets have both?** Asked 2026-08-01 — "we abstracted it to 1, but now we have spin and magnetic fields we may be able to do that more accurately, or happy to stay as is". Recorded so nobody re-opens it without new information. **The premise does not hold yet: planets have DERIVED spin and field, stars do NOT.** [[B9]](b) established there is no stellar rotation model anywhere, and a star's `mag_gauss` is a rule-pack RANGE rolled at generation rather than derived from anything. The ingredients for a spin-driven stellar model are exactly what the banked HR-diagram phase would provide. **And the current model is considerably better than "abstracted to 1" suggests.** `radiationOutput` is rule-pack DATA per class, not a constant — `static/rulepacks/starter-sf/stars.json` carries G `[0.8, 1.2]`, F `[1.5, 3]`, K `[0.5, 1.5]`, M `[0.8, 1500]`; Sol reads 1 because it is the reference. `photonParticleSplit` (`radiation.ts:28-43`) already varies by spectral class, O/B 95:5 through M 78:22, which is the right shape since cool dwarfs are wind- and flare-dominated. `flareActivity` is already DERIVED from class and system age (`SystemProcessor.ts:79`, `physics/stellar-evolution.ts`), already feeds the particle channel via `FLARE_PARTICLE_WEIGHT` (`radiation.ts:144`), and already raises `hazard/flaring` above 0.4. **THE ARGUMENT FOR LEAVING IT, which is the part worth keeping:** real stellar activity IS rotation-driven, but stellar rotation is itself a function of class and age — stars spin down by magnetic braking, which is precisely WHY the age-activity relation exists (gyrochronology runs the same inference backwards). So `flareActivity(class, age)` is already that causal chain with the intermediate elided. Inserting spin would derive spin from class and age, then derive activity from spin, and arrive at the same number: more mechanism, no more accuracy. **Where it WOULD genuinely gain, and the trigger for revisiting:** close binaries tidally spun UP and therefore hyperactive far beyond their age (RS CVn systems run orders of magnitude above the age prediction), and young systems before rotation converges. Both are real and both are currently invisible — and both need the HR-diagram work regardless. **Revisit with the HR phase, not before**, at which point stellar spin becomes a derived property worth showing a GM in its own right. Related: [[B9]], [[project_sse_v2_hr_stellar]]. | checked — no change, revisit with the HR phase |
| B27 | **`orbitalRadiation` names a place it does not describe: Earth's reads 653 Sv/yr, which would tell a GM that low orbit is instantly fatal.** Noticed 2026-08-01 while verifying [[B22]], which is otherwise clean. The figure is `652,964.7` mSv/yr for Earth. That is defensible AT THE BELT INNER EDGE — the inner proton belt genuinely is a lethal place to linger — but "orbital radiation" reads as "the dose in orbit", and the ISS at 400 km takes about **150 mSv/yr**, roughly four thousand times less, because it flies BELOW the inner belt except over the South Atlantic Anomaly. A GM planning a station or a parking orbit is being told the wrong thing by four orders of magnitude. **Same fault class as [[A33]]**, which was the same week: a number correct for a reference point that its label does not name. It is not a physics error and [[B22]]'s numbers should not be re-derived — Earth's inner edge at 1.1982 R_E matches the measured ~1.2 R_E, and Jupiter's 747 Sv/day at the cloud tops is right. **It is a question of WHERE the second figure is quoted, and it needs answering per body type rather than globally.** For an airless body the orbital and surface environments genuinely coincide (Io's two figures agree, correctly). For a giant, the cloud-top figure is the useful one and is what was asked for. For a MAGNETISED body with an atmosphere, the interesting number is not the belt peak at all — it is the dose in a LOW orbit beneath the belt, which is where anything actually parks. Options: name the reference altitude in the label; quote a below-the-belt figure for magnetised bodies; or give the belt peak its own clearly hazardous name and keep "orbital" for somewhere a ship would sit. **Whatever is chosen must survive [[B20]]**, which will decompose these figures by source and cause and needs each one to mean a definite place. Related: [[B22]], [[A33]], [[B20]], [[B21]]. **FIXED v2.1.382-beta. CHOSE OPTION (a) — NAME THE PLACE — NOT (c), and the justification matters because (c) is a new physics model in disguise.** Quoting a below-the-belt figure needs an ALTITUDE parameter the engine does not have, against a belt law that is a single peak-and-scale-length fit with one calibration anchor. It would swap a 4,000× overshoot for an undershoot on a number nothing could check, and this entry itself says the physics is right and the question is where the figure is quoted. **[[B26]] already answered this exact class of fault the same way** — the number is meaningful, the label was wrong — and naming it is what [[B20]] will need, since every figure has to mean a definite place before anything can decompose it by cause. **THE ALTITUDE IS DERIVED, NOT A CONSTANT:** `(beltInnerEdgeRadii − 1) × radiusKm`, i.e. where that world's own atmosphere stops absorbing. **Earth now reads `Radiation (in the belts, from ~1,262 km)`.** **PER BODY TYPE, as demanded:** *airless* — the belt edge IS the surface, the two figures coincide and the second row was already suppressed (Io, Luna, Mercury, Europa, Ganymede); *giant* — "above the cloud tops", B22's wording untouched; *magnetised with air* — "in the belts, from ~N km"; *ring* — the ring plane, one row ([[B26]]). **The tag follows:** `hazard/orbital-radiation` is relabelled **"Radiation belts"** and its description now states it is the belt inner edge and NOT the dose at an altitude a ship chooses, with the ISS's 150 mSv/yr as the contrast — read it as "there is a hazardous shell around this world", not "orbit is lethal". **REUSE:** `orbitalRadiationPlace` sits beside `radiationPlace` in `physics/radiation.ts`, one home for "which place does this figure describe"; the bucketing is untouched and still goes through `radiationHazardBucket`, so no second thresholding was added. **Derived diff: UNCHANGED, which is the point — no dose was re-derived.** **NOT DONE, and it is the honest residual:** a genuine low-orbit dose still does not exist. It needs an altitude-parameterised dose function and a second calibration anchor, and it belongs with [[B20]]. | fixed v2.1.382-beta |
| B28 | **Io is tagged `Surface · Irradiation: low` while taking 12,750 Sv/yr — the tag is bound to a space-weathering proxy, not to the hazard.** Reported 2026-08-01 after [[B17]]/[[B22]] landed: "the tag generation system hasn't caught up — are your tag rules bound to the base solar radiation values rather than the newly implemented [belt]?" Yes. **VERIFIED:** `deriveIrradiationDose(teqK, magShield, surfaceAgeGyr)` (`radiation.ts:17`) takes exactly those three arguments and never sees `surfaceRadiation`, so it cannot know a belt exists; `SystemProcessor.ts:862-868` buckets its output into `surface/irradiation` at thresholds 0.05 / 0.2. **BUT THE QUANTITY IS NOT WRONG AND MUST NOT BE "FIXED" BY FEEDING IT THE BELT.** Its own comment states its purpose: it is the driver of THOLIN darkening (`docs/dev/geo-foundations.md` Foundation 4) — cumulative space weathering, stellar UV plus a galactic-cosmic-ray floor, times the unshielded fraction, times how long the surface has been exposed. Io's value is 0 because `ageFactor = surfaceAgeGyr / 4.5` and Io is resurfaced continuously by volcanism, so its surface has accumulated no weathering at all. For what it measures, zero is CORRECT. **The fault is that an APPEARANCE DRIVER is being published as a HAZARD READING.** A GM reading "Irradiation: low" on the most irradiated surface in the Solar System is being actively misled, and a player standing there dies. The user-facing tag should be bucketed off `surfaceRadiation` — which now includes the belt — while `irradiationDose` keeps driving tholins under a name that says what it is. Check Europa too: it reads 0.002 and is tagged low while taking 1,437 Sv/yr. **A SEPARATE AND LEGITIMATE PHYSICS QUESTION, worth its own consideration rather than being bundled into the tag fix:** should the belt ALSO feed the weathering model? Europa's real surface discolouration IS radiolytic — sulphur from Io's torus, processed by exactly the particles B17 now models — so there is a genuine case that a magnetospheric term belongs in the tholin/weathering chain as well. That is a physics change with visual consequences and it needs its own before/after diff; the tag fix does not and should not wait for it. **THIRD INSTANCE OF ONE FAMILY THIS WEEK** — see [[A33]] (a relative ratio printed beside an absolute dose) and [[B27]] (the belt peak labelled as "orbital"). The radiation model has grown several distinct quantities and the presentation layer treats them as interchangeable. Whoever takes [[B20]] should settle the VOCABULARY once — what each quantity measures, where, and in what units — rather than fixing the three labels one at a time. Related: [[B17]], [[B22]], [[A33]], [[B27]], [[B20]]. **FIXED v2.1.357-beta, and the entry's own warning was the right call: `irradiationDose` was not touched.** **The gap turned out to be an ABSENCE, not a mislabelling** — [[A33]] had already renamed `surface/irradiation`'s label to "Space weathering" at v2.1.344, so the word "Irradiation" was gone from the block. What remained was worse in a quieter way: there was **NO TAG AT ALL** carrying the annual dose, so a GM scanning or filtering tags on Io saw "Space weathering: low" and nothing else, with the real figure living only in an info-block row. **NEW TAG `hazard/radiation`, bucketed off `surfaceRadiation`** (which carries the belt), emitted beside the weathering tag. Its buckets are reference points rather than round numbers, and the boundaries are stated in `radiation.ts`: `background` under 10 mSv/yr (Earth's natural background is ~2.4), `elevated` past the 20 mSv/yr occupational limit, `high` 100-1000 (a real mission dose - Mars 214 from Curiosity RAD), `severe` to 1e5 (a lethal total in weeks to months), `lethal` at 100 Sv/yr and up (days or less). **THE RESULT ON THE TWO BODIES THE ENTRY NAMED:** Io `hazard/radiation=lethal` beside `surface/irradiation=low`, and **Europa the same** - both readings correct, because they answer different questions, and now both visible. Ganymede `severe`, Mercury `severe`, Enceladus `severe`, Mars/Luna/Phobos `high`, Callisto `elevated`, Earth/Venus/Titan/Triton/Pluto `background`. **ONE FUNCTION, NOT TWO.** `bodyFacts.ts` already had its own `radBand` bucketing the SAME quantity three ways (low/moderate/high at 5 and 100) - a second answer to one question, and it put **Mars at 214 mSv/yr in the same bucket as Io at 13 million**, a range of sixty thousand described by one word. The row and the tag now share `radiationHazardBucket`, so the info block reads "lethal - 36 Sv/day" for Io and "high - 214 mSv/y" for Mars. Pinned by a new test that asserts the two do not get the same word. **GATED TO SOLID SURFACES**, deliberately: a giant has no ground, so its figure is a 1-bar reading and a "surface hazard" tag would repeat exactly the category error [[B18]] fixed and [[B22]] labelled around. Giants keep their row and get no tag. **DERIVED DIFF: PURE ADDITION.** 24 bodies gain `hazard/radiation` and **nothing else changed at all** - no dose, no weathering value, no other tag. **NOT DONE, and it is the separate physics question this entry flagged: should the belt ALSO feed the weathering model?** Untouched here, on purpose. Europa's real surface discolouration IS radiolytic - sulphur from Io's torus, processed by the very particles [[B17]] now models - so there is a genuine case that a magnetospheric term belongs in the tholin chain. It is a physics change with visual consequences and needs its own before/after diff; see the unnumbered capture at the bottom. **Also left:** the five-word bucket vocabulary here is new and does not match the low/moderate/high used elsewhere - that is [[B20]]/[[B29]]'s to settle, not a reason to hold this. | fixed v2.1.357-beta |
| B29 | **AUDIT: does every tag report the quantity its name claims? Sweep all 41 tag-emission sites.** Raised 2026-08-01 after [[B28]] — "audit and validate this won't happen more, or improve instructions?" Both; this is the audit half, and the instruction half is already in the standing rules at the foot of this file. **The surface is BOUNDED and countable, so this is one session rather than an open-ended sweep:** 41 `tags.push` sites — 32 in `core/SystemProcessor.ts`, then `physics/stability.ts` (2), `physics/surfaceTemperature.ts` (2), `physics/accrete-adapter.ts` (2), `physics/cloudDecks.ts` (1), `physics/resonance.ts` (1), `physics/reasonsToVisit.ts` (1). **ONE QUESTION PER SITE:** what quantity is this tag bucketed off, and is that the quantity a reader would think the tag describes? Record the answer for every site even where it is fine — a table of "tag / source quantity / what a reader assumes / verdict" is the deliverable, because the value is as much in the sites that are CLEAN as in the ones that are not. The next person should never have to re-derive this. **Precedent for expecting real finds:** the equivalent sweep on paired figures during [[A5]] turned up [[B8]], a genuine physics fault putting 105 of 420 bodies outside their own stated range. Three of this family are already known — [[A33]], [[B27]], [[B28]] — and they were each found by accident rather than by looking. **THE DURABLE HALF, and it is the point of doing this at all:** for every mismatch found, add a CONSISTENCY TEST rather than only fixing the label, because a fixed label drifts again the moment the underlying physics moves. The tests are cheap and concrete — for instance, no body with `surfaceRadiation` above a lethal threshold may carry `surface/irradiation: low`. That form of assertion, one per pair the audit finds, is what stops the whole family recurring. **Do NOT fix physics here.** If a tag is wrong because the quantity behind it is wrong, that is a new B item — write it up unnumbered and carry on. This audit changes labels, buckets and tests, not models. Related: [[B28]], [[A33]], [[B27]], [[A5]], [[B8]]. **DONE v2.1.366-beta. THE TABLE IS `docs/dev/tag-audit.md`** — every emission site, including the clean ones, so nobody re-derives it. **FIVE FINDINGS, four fixed as labels/buckets and one recorded as a model question.** **(1) `aurora/*` published a raw 0..1 float** — [[A35]]'s own named case, "Brilliant aurora: 0.78", a number on a scale nothing states. The tier is ALREADY in the key, so the value adds precision the renderer needs and nothing a reader can use: it is now suppressed for readers and kept in the data. **(2) `thermal/self-luminous` published a bare Kelvin figure** and **(3) `feature/polar-vortex` a bare count** — now `1,500 K` and `6-sided`. **(4) `volatiles/ices` packed a `+`-delimited list into one value** (`carbon-dioxide+nitrogen+water+methane`) — banned by the architecture doc's own idiom, and unreadable as a chip. Now one tag per species, which is the pattern the doc sanctions for a body that has several of a thing. **(5) THREE TAGS WERE EMITTED WITH NO REGISTRY ENTRY AT ALL** — `high-humidity`, `biosignature`, `exotic-biology` — so they reached a reader as bare title-cased words with no description. Worse, the last two fire on the SAME trigger for the SAME gas, so a world showed "Biosignature" beside "Exotic biology" with nothing to say they were one observation seen twice. Registered, and the descriptions now distinguish them. **THE DURABLE HALF — `src/lib/tags/tagConsistency.spec.ts`, five assertions over every bundled body:** no bare unitless number may reach a reader (either the presentation layer gives it a unit or suppresses it — a NEW numeric value with neither fails the suite); no delimited mini-format in a value; the hazard word must match the dose it claims to describe ([[B28]]'s example, generalised); and **every emitted key must be REGISTERED**, which is the assertion that caught finding (5) and would have caught it years ago. **The bodies are PROCESSED before auditing, not read out of the bundled JSON** — a stored starmap is a snapshot that may predate the current engine by many versions, so auditing it as-is audits history. **ONE EXISTING TEST WAS WRONG AND IS CORRECTED:** `save-roundtrip.test.ts` asserted that a tag KEY never appears twice on a body. The architecture doc explicitly sanctions the opposite ("Multiple tags may share a key where a body legitimately has several of a thing... dedupe by (key, species)"), and `structure/cloud-deck` has always done it — the assertion passed only because no test body had two of anything. Now keyed on (key, value), which is the invariant it meant. **RECORDED, NOT FIXED, because it is a model question and this audit does not change models:** `tidal/volcanism` (a tidal peak TEMPERATURE threshold in `surfaceTemperature.ts`) and `geology/volcanic-tidal` (the `deriveGeoActivity` REGIME) are two independent answers to "is this world tidally volcanic?". They agree today — Io is the only body carrying either and it carries both — but nothing enforces it, and [[B1]] is the precedent for what happens next. Also recorded: `atmosphere/breathable` is written once at generation and never re-derived, while `breathable-human` is a rule-pack trigger re-evaluated every pass, so editing a world's air updates one and not the other. Both are in the table. **NOT AUDITED, deliberately:** the provenance tags a concurrent session is adding on the manual-creation route (a new `spin/*` namespace appeared mid-sweep). Left alone; they should get the same one-question treatment when that lands. | fixed v2.1.366-beta — table at docs/dev/tag-audit.md |
| B30 | **Express the radiation hazard as TIME TO HARM, because that is what a non-technical GM can use.** Requested 2026-08-02, refining [[B28]] which had just landed. **Note first that the tag now EXISTS** — B28 (v2.1.357) added `hazard/radiation`, bucketed off `surfaceRadiation` so it carries the belt, gated to solid surfaces, and unified with `bodyFacts`, which had been bucketing the same quantity differently and calling Mars and Io both "high" sixty thousand times apart. Current values: `background` (<10 mSv/yr) · `elevated` (past the 20 mSv/yr occupational limit) · `high` (100-1000) · `severe` (to 1e5) · `lethal` (100 Sv/yr and up). Boundaries are real reference points, not round numbers. **THE ASK IS THAT THE VOCABULARY MAP ONTO SURVIVAL TIME** — extreme meaning minutes to hours, high meaning days — so a GM who does not read sieverts still knows what happens to a character standing there. **AND IT IS DERIVABLE, NOT A TABLE, which is the whole reason it is worth doing properly.** A median lethal acute dose is about 5 Sv, so time-to-LD50 is simply `5 Sv / dose rate`: Io 36 Sv/day gives about **3 hours**; Europa 3.8 Sv/day about 1.3 days; Ganymede 0.12 Sv/day about 40 days; Mars 214 mSv/yr about 23 years; Earth 2.3 mSv/yr about two thousand. One division on a figure the engine already holds, and the LD50 constant belongs in rule-pack DATA where a campaign can argue with it. **Where it should surface, in the order the request put them:** (1) improve the HOVER TEXT on the value in the data block — cheapest, no vocabulary change, and it can say the sentence outright ("a lethal dose in about three hours"). (2) Consider whether the TAG can carry it too — stated as "hard to bundle in a tag, although that would be ideal". It probably can: the tag value could stay a bucket word while the presentation layer renders the time beside it, or the bucket words themselves could be re-cut on survival-time boundaries rather than on dose boundaries. Decide which, and say why. **CAUTION — do not silently re-cut the buckets and leave the numbers behind.** The existing boundaries are anchored to real references (the occupational limit, Curiosity's Mars measurement); re-cutting on survival time must keep those anchors landing in sensible buckets, or a body's tag will move without its physics moving. Check every bundled body before and after. **Long-exposure honesty:** time-to-LD50 is an ACUTE model and is nonsense at background levels — "2,000 years" is arithmetic, not a prediction, because chronic low dose kills by cancer risk rather than by acute syndrome. Below some rate the phrasing must change to a chronic framing rather than quoting a number nobody will live to test. **PROPOSED VALUE, 2026-08-02: make the tag's value DAYS TO LETHALITY — "about 0.2 for Io" — and bucket anything beyond 50 YEARS as background, on the grounds that "it COULD kill you but so could many other things".** The arithmetic checks: 5 Sv / 36 Sv per day = 0.14 days, and 0.2 falls out of a slightly higher LD50, so the constant is the only thing to settle and it belongs in rule-pack data. The 50-year cut-off is a good boundary for exactly the reason given — it is where the acute model stops meaning anything and chronic risk takes over, which is the honest limit noted above. **ONE TENSION TO RESOLVE RATHER THAN IGNORE:** a raw number as a tag value is the fault [[A35]] flagged, and the architecture doc's own idiom is that continuous physics should be BUCKETED into tag values. **The synthesis that satisfies both: bucket names that are TIME WORDS** — `hours` / `days` / `weeks` / `months` / `background` — with the precise figure carried in the hover text and the data block. That is bucketed per the idiom and time-expressed per the request, and a GM reads "Radiation: days" without parsing anything. If a number really is wanted in the tag itself, say why and note that it makes [[A35]] a wont-fix rather than an oversight. Related: [[B28]], [[B20]], [[A33]], [[B29]], [[A35]]. **FIXED v2.1.359-beta. THE TENSION IS RESOLVED IN FAVOUR OF TIME WORDS, and [[A35]] stands — no raw float went into a tag value.** The bucket names ARE the survival time, derived (`LD50 / dose rate`) rather than tabulated, with the precise figure beside the dose in the data block. A GM reads "Radiation hazard: hours" and needs to parse nothing. **Why not days-to-lethality as the value, as proposed:** it is a continuous quantity, the architecture doc's idiom buckets those, and the number would have had to be read against an invisible scale (is 0.2 bad? is 40?) — which is the very thing this item exists to remove. The word does the work; the number is one line away for anyone who wants it. **THE LADDER, and it has SEVEN rungs rather than five because the honesty limit needs two of them:** `hours` (under a day) · `days` (under a week) · `weeks` (under a month) · `months` (under a year) · `years` (under 50) · `chronic` (past 50 years to an acute LD50, but above the 20 mSv/yr occupational limit — real long-term cancer risk, no acute threat) · `background` (at or below it). **THE ACUTE MODEL IS NOT ALLOWED TO SPEAK PAST 50 YEARS.** It describes radiation sickness, and "Earth: 2,000 years" is arithmetic rather than a prediction. Past that boundary the ladder stops quoting time and changes framing, and the spelled-out sentence in the data block is DROPPED entirely rather than printed — pinned by a test that Earth's row contains no "lethal dose" phrase at all. **BOTH ANCHORS SURVIVE THE RE-CUT, checked rather than assumed.** The 20 mSv/yr occupational limit is now the `chronic`/`background` line itself, so it is more load-bearing than before, not less. **Curiosity's Mars figure lands on `years` (~23)** — which is the honest framing anyway: a Mars surface dose is a mission-planning problem measured in years, not a thing that kills you on arrival. **EVERY BUNDLED BODY DIFFED, and the direction holds throughout:** Io `lethal` -> **`hours`** (~3.4 h), Europa `lethal` -> **`days`** (~1.3 d), Ganymede `severe` -> `months` (~40 d), Mimas `severe` -> `months`, Mercury/Enceladus `severe` -> `years`, Mars/Luna/Phobos/Deimos/Tethys `high` -> `years`, Callisto/Dione `elevated` -> `chronic`, Earth/Venus/Titan/Triton/Pluto unchanged at `background`. **Nothing numeric moved anywhere: 0 changed values across the whole fixture** — this is a vocabulary change and the diff proves it. **The LD50 constant is rule-pack DATA** (`generation_parameters.radiation_ld50_sv`, default 5 Sv), so a campaign can argue with it, and the info block's band and the tag both call one function so they cannot drift apart. **Left:** the ladder's floor is `hours` and does not distinguish Io's 3 hours from Jupiter-orbit's 9 minutes — deliberate, since "minutes to hours" was the requested reading for the extreme end and the figure is printed beside the word. | fixed v2.1.359-beta |
| B31 | **THE INVERSE AUDIT: what does the engine DERIVE that a GM would want, and that carries no tag at all?** Asked 2026-08-02 — "anything else we could add tags to that resides in data but could be in tags". **This is the mirror of [[B29]] and should be run by the same session in the same pass.** B29 asks "does every tag report the quantity its name claims"; this asks "what quantity deserves a tag and has not got one". Both are one sweep over the same ground and the answers inform each other. **The precedent is [[B28]], which was exactly this fault:** the gap was an ABSENCE — nothing anywhere carried the annual dose, so a GM filtering tags on Io saw "Space weathering: low" and no indication its surface takes 36 Sv a day. That fault is invisible to any audit that only inspects tags that exist. **Method:** enumerate the DERIVED fields on a processed body (the generated `tests/output/solar-system-derived.json` is the complete list, and it is regenerated on every vitest run so it cannot go stale), and for each ask two questions — would a GM act on this at the table, and is it reachable as a tag? Deliver a table of field / has-a-tag / should-it / verdict, covering the fields that are FINE as well as the gaps, so nobody re-derives it. **Candidates already visible, offered as a starting list rather than the answer:** `orbitalRadiation` and `beltInnerEdgeRadii` from [[B22]] (parking a ship is a real decision and nothing surfaces it); the ascent delta-v shown in the stat block (it decides whether a party can leave, and is arguably the single most actionable figure on a rocky world); `radiationShieldingAtmo` and `radiationShieldingMag` (they explain WHY a dose is what it is); the surface-radiation min/max range. **The discipline that keeps this from becoming tag soup:** a tag earns its place only if a GM would FILTER or DECIDE on it. Everything else belongs in the data block, and the standing rule about bucketing continuous physics applies to anything that does earn one. Say explicitly which candidates you rejected and why — a short rejected list is as valuable as the additions. Related: [[B29]], [[B28]], [[B22]], [[A35]]. **TABLE DELIVERED AND THE TWO CLEAR WINS BUILT, v2.1.359-beta. The rest is recorded below so nobody re-derives it.** **THE DISCIPLINE APPLIED:** a tag earns its place only if a GM would FILTER or DECIDE on it; an explainer belongs in the data block. **ADDED (2):** **`flight/ascent`** — bucketed `loDeltaVBudget_ms`, `trivial` (<2 km/s, a small craft hops off) · `moderate` (<5) · `hard` (<15) · `extreme`. Anchors: Luna 1.9 trivial, Mars 4.1 moderate, **Earth 10.4 hard**, Venus 29.5 extreme. It decides whether a party can leave, and it had no tag at all. **Emitted in PASS 4, not pass 3** — the budget is written in `processFlightDynamics`, so a tag in classification would have read the previous run's number, which is the whole of [[B13]]. Gated to solid surfaces ([[B18]]: there is nothing to ascend FROM on a giant). **`hazard/orbital-radiation`** — the same survival-time ladder for the space a ship parks in, **and only when it is NEWS**: the tag appears solely when its BUCKET WORD differs from the surface one. That gate matters — a naive "1.5x bigger" test gave Titan a second tag reading `background` beside a first reading `background`. Across all of Sol exactly **two bodies qualify: Earth (`background` on the ground, `days` in orbit — the Van Allen belts) and Venus (`background` / `years`)**, which is precisely the surprise worth surfacing. **REJECTED, with reasons — this list is the deliverable as much as the additions.** `beltInnerEdgeRadii`: a geometry figure in host radii; a GM decides "park high or low", and the thing they decide on is the orbital DOSE, which now has a tag. An input, not a decision. `radiationShieldingAtmo` / `radiationShieldingMag`: they explain WHY a dose is what it is, and the causes are already tagged in their own right (`magnetic/*`, atmospheric pressure). A GM filters on the outcome, not the mechanism. Data block. `surfaceRadiationMin/Max`: a variation, not a decision, and already printed inside the row it belongs to ([[A33]]'s rule). `calculatedGravity_ms2`: a genuine table decision, but "0.38 g" is already legible to anyone — the whole case for the radiation tags is that sieverts are NOT. Adding a tag would be tag soup for no legibility gain. `equilibriumTempK` / `temperatureRangeK` / `temperatureProfile`: tagged already, via the surface-temperature profile tags. `orbitalStability`, `geoActivity`, `volatiles`, `tidallyLocked`, `magnetism`, `habitabilityScore`, `hydrosphere.layers`: all ALREADY have tags (`stability/*`, `geology/*`, `volatiles/*`, `orbit/*`, `magnetic/*`, `habitability/*`, `structure/*`) — recorded as FINE so the next sweep does not re-open them. `albedoBreakdown`, `apparentColor`, `auroraEmitters`, `oblateness`, `classification`, `atmosphere.molarMassKg`, `atmosphere.scaleHeightKm`, `photonRadiation`/`particleRadiation`: appearance drivers and model internals. Not table decisions. **LEFT AS ARGUABLE for a later pass, deliberately not built:** `propulsiveLandBudget_ms` / `aerobrakeLandBudget_ms` (landing cost is as real a decision as ascent, but the two-number aerobrake-vs-propulsive choice does not bucket into one word without deciding which the GM means — that needs a call, not a threshold); and `orbitalBoundaries.minLeoKm` (where the lowest safe orbit sits — decision-shaped, but its units and its relationship to [[B22]]'s belt inner edge want settling together, which is [[B20]]'s job). **DERIVED DIFF: 24 bodies gain `flight/ascent`, 2 gain `hazard/orbital-radiation`, and ZERO numeric values moved.** | fixed v2.1.359-beta — table above, two additions built, rest recorded |
| B32 | **"How deep do I have to dig?" — derive the SHIELDING REQUIRED to bring a body's dose down to background.** Requested 2026-08-02 alongside [[B30]]: how far underground to not be hit, or how much aluminium/titanium hull thickness — **target explicitly stated as reaching BACKGROUND**. This is the most directly playable radiation figure yet proposed: it converts a dose a GM cannot act on into a decision a party makes ("you need two metres of regolith over the habitat"). **THE TARGET IS ALREADY DEFINED, so nothing needs inventing:** [[B28]] set `background` at under 10 mSv/yr. Required attenuation is therefore just `surfaceRadiation / 10 mSv/yr`. **AND THE MATHS IS ALREADY IN THE ENGINE, INVERTED.** `radiation.ts:318` computes `atmoTransmission = Math.exp(-shieldingScore * pressure_bar)` — an exponential attenuation against column depth. This item solves the same expression for the DEPTH instead of the dose, exactly as [[B21]]'s zone radius falls out of [[B17]]'s law. **Work in AREAL DENSITY (g/cm2) internally, present as THICKNESS per material.** Areal density is the physical quantity — it is why Earth is safe under about 1030 g/cm2 of air — and thickness is areal density divided by the material's density. Materials belong in rule-pack DATA (density, and any per-material effectiveness), so a campaign can add its own; note the bundled Sol campaign already writes of "Xenonite hull stock", which is precisely the case data-driven materials serve. Reference densities: regolith about 1.5-2.0, aluminium 2.7, titanium 4.5, water 1.0 g/cm3. **CALIBRATION ANCHOR, and it is a good one because it is independently documented:** Mars at 214 mSv/yr needs roughly a factor of 21, which against a GCR attenuation length of order 100 g/cm2 is about 300 g/cm2, or **roughly two metres of regolith** — which is what real martian habitat studies call for. If the model produces that for Mars it is behaving. Io then wants something like eight metres, and the gulf between those two numbers is the whole value of the feature. **THE PHYSICS TRAP, and a naive implementation WILL get it wrong: denser is not automatically better.** Against the energetic ELECTRONS that dominate a giant's belt, high-Z materials such as titanium or lead generate bremsstrahlung — secondary X-rays — so per unit mass they can shield WORSE than aluminium, and worse again than hydrogen-rich materials like water or polyethylene. Ranking materials by density alone would tell a GM to build Europa's shelter out of the worst available choice. Either carry a per-material effectiveness factor in the rule pack, or restrict the answer to hydrogen-rich and low-Z materials and say why. **Also note the answer differs by SOURCE**, which is an argument for doing this after [[B20]]: the depth needed against a stellar particle flux is not the depth needed against belt electrons, and a single number that averages them is a number for nowhere. **FRAMING, STATED BY THE REQUESTER AND IT SETS THE PRECISION BAR: "just handy guides, as radiation is tough to visualise."** This and [[B30]] are LEGIBILITY AIDS, not engineering calculations. Nobody is designing a real habitat from them; a GM is deciding whether the party can shelter here. So prefer the round and memorable to the precise — "about two metres of rock", "a lethal dose in hours" — and do not chase a second significant figure that costs clarity. **The one thing that precision licence does NOT excuse is being wrong in DIRECTION.** An approximate guide is fine; a backwards one is worse than none, because a GM will act on it. The titanium case above is exactly that: a "handy guide" that ranks materials by density would send a party to build Europa's shelter out of the worst thing available. Approximate freely, invert nothing. Related: [[B28]], [[B30]], [[B20]], [[B21]], [[B17]]. | captured |
| B33 | **A 751 C helium giant is tagged "Life-support resupply", "Water/ice refuelling" and "Water ice". Three tags, one missing gate.** Reported 2026-08-02 on Grolith — 112 Earth masses, 86.6 bar He/H2 envelope, 751 C mean surface, `planet/helium`. **VERIFIED, all three rules live in `physics/reasonsToVisit.ts`.** `frontier/life-support` (`:121`) fires on `hasO2` OR (`hydro === 'water'` AND `hasAtmo`); `frontier/fuel-depot` (`:119`) on `makeup.ice >= 0.2` OR `hydro === 'water'`; `resource/water-ice` (`:98`) on `hydro === 'water'` OR `hydroCover >= 0.1` OR `makeup.ice >= 0.3` OR the icy-shell tag. A giant has an atmosphere by definition and enough ice in its bulk makeup to clear those thresholds, so all three fire. **THE COMMON FAULT: these rules describe SURFACE-ACCESSIBLE resources but test BULK COMPOSITION, with no gate on whether the material is reachable or in a usable phase.** Water in a 751 C, 86.6 bar helium envelope is supercritical vapour mixed through a planet-sized atmosphere; it is not ice, not a lake, and not something a ship scoops on the way past. **THE FIX IS ALREADY HALF-BUILT AND IT IS THE ELEGANT PART: `isGiant` EXISTS AS A RULE FIELD** (`:55`, "Gas/ice giant (or gas fraction >= 0.4)") and is used POSITIVELY by `resource/helium-3` (`:95`) and `frontier/gas-skimming` (`:120`), both of which correctly REQUIRE it. **No rule anywhere EXCLUDES giants**, so every surface-oriented rule fires on one. Adding that exclusion to the surface-resource and surface-frontier rules is a small, uniform change against a field that already exists — and Grolith keeps "Gas-giant refuelling" and "Helium-3", which are right. **A SECOND, MORE GENERAL GATE WORTH CONSIDERING, because it catches cases `isGiant` will not:** a `frontier/*` tag is a claim that somewhere is worth STOPPING AT, and the same panel already prints the counter-argument — Grolith's surface-to-low-orbit budget is **52.3 km/s**. A place you cannot practically land on and leave is not a resupply stop whatever it contains, and that delta-v is already derived. Gating frontier tags on it would also catch dense non-giants that `isGiant` misses. **CHECK THE WHOLE `frontier/*` AND `resource/*` FAMILY, not just these three** — the same bulk-composition-versus-accessibility confusion will be in others. **Note the deliberate history on `resource/water-ice`:** its comment records that it "was wrongly capped to frozen worlds <250K", so a temperature cap was REMOVED on purpose to let liquid-water worlds yield ice. That removal was probably right; do not simply reinstate a cap. The missing test is accessibility and phase, not temperature alone. **THIS IS A LIVE FIND FOR [[B29]]** — `reasonsToVisit.ts` is one of the 41 tag-emission sites that audit covers, and this is precisely the question it asks. Route it there. Related: [[B29]], [[B4]], [[B18]], [[B22]]. **FIXED v2.1.368-beta.** **Swept the whole family as asked, by running the tagger over all 366 bundled bodies and listing what fires on the 59 with no surface.** THREE MORE were wrong for the same reason — `resource/heavy-metals`, `resource/exotic-crystals` and `resource/organics` are mining and farming claims — and `platinum-group`, `rare-earths`, `fissiles` and `diamonds` are the same shape and would fire on the right giant. **All ten are now gated.** What a giant KEEPS was checked rather than assumed: helium-3, gas-skimming, deuterium, aerobraking and gravity-assist are byte-identical before and after. **Before, across the 59 surfaceless bodies: fuel-depot 18, water-ice 15, life-support 1 — 34 false claims. After: zero, with all 159 legitimate water-ice tags on surfaced bodies untouched.** **WHICH GATE — NEITHER OF THE TWO THIS ENTRY PROPOSED, and both were measured before being rejected.** (a) `isGiant` reads well but is "classes include a giant type OR gas ≥ 0.4", and the bundled data holds bodies carrying BOTH a rocky and a giant class — `planet/desert + planet/cloudless-gas-giant` at gas 0.00, `planet/ice + planet/ice-giant` at gas 0.00 — so gating on it would strip surface resources from bodies whose own composition says they have a surface. (b) **The delta-v gate is the better IDEA — accessibility is exactly the concept these rules are missing — but it cannot do THIS job**, because for a body with no surface the figure is derived at the notional 1-bar radius and is as fictional as the surface gravity [[B18]] found there. Measured over all 366 bundled bodies, **the giants' surface-to-LEO budgets run 9.1 to 1163 km/s: the low end is Earth's**, so no threshold excludes giants without excluding Earth. Using it here would build on the fiction B18 identified. So: `makeup.gas < 0.5`, the same test B18 and [[B25]] use — classification, habitability and the reasons to visit now answer "does this body have a surface" identically. **No temperature cap was reinstated on `resource/water-ice`**; the missing test was never temperature and a 300 K ocean world still yields ice. **Grolith itself is not bundled** — it is a user's body; the identical fault was present on 34 bundled tags, which is what the numbers above measure. **`tests/output/solar-system-derived.json`: 4 lines, ONE body, ONE tag — Saturn loses `frontier/fuel-depot` and keeps helium-3, gas-skimming, aerobraking and gravity-assist.** Docs followed in the same commit (physics page + tags-guide). **Still worth doing separately, and NOT done here: the delta-v gate as its own concept** — "a place you cannot practically land on and leave is not a resupply stop" is a real second question for dense NON-giants, where the figure is honest. Related: [[B18]], [[B25]], [[B29]]. | fixed v2.1.368-beta |

The WIZARD path does set a stellar tilt, but only inside `applyKnobBias`, which `generateSystemFromConfig` calls only `if (config.knobs && !config.emptyPlanets)` — so a wizard run with no knobs also produces none, and its PLANETS never get one on any path. The manual add-a-body route in `SystemView.svelte:478-505` is the only place that sets planetary tilt, by die-roll.
